- **What switchboard does:** stores the handoff and sends that one message. **What it doesn't:** fetch the PR or MR, hold a token, post, comment, approve or merge anything (§37.1).
- **Tests:** `handoff_plan` and `render_handoff`, including the person decision marker, message hygiene and size bound (unit); the header's words for every blocked case with their singular and plural, ready, done, and the nudge's window, in node (`test_web_app_behavior.py`, scenario `board_handoff_header`); refusal before confirmed fixes, selected-agent ownership, one message and one handoff (integration); the three header states in light, dark and on a phone with the words updating as agents settle items and no Hand off in any lane, Tab order and focus kept through redraws, no highlight for a board that opens ready, disabled button, dialog preview and agent choice, Escape focus, and phone layout (e2e); and Close board keeping its focus through a members frame and a review frame, and Shift+Tab from it going back to the header's link, not the top of the page (e2e, #430).

### 37.8 A person starts a review: `/review` (#248)
- **The command:** `/review <url> [note]` (§10), `human_cli` like `/catchup`, since all it does is open a board and post a message as the person. The URL is checked as the tool's `open` checks it (`reviews.url`); the note is cleaned like chat text. An agent's `say("/review …")` is posted literally, like any command.
- **The board:** `Boards.person_open` opens the room's board for the URL with the person as `opened_by`, through the same `_open_board` as the tool, so the notice and the `review` event hold. A URL whose board was closed gets it back with its items and history, an open board for the same URL stays as it is (the request is asked again), and another URL opens a second board beside the first (§37.14), up to the room's cap. It publishes the room's boards at once.
- **The message:** one human chat message (`review_request`): an `@mention` of every agent in the room, then switchboard's own request to review the URL together on the board through `review(action="raise")`, `ask` and `show`, and `Note: <note>` last. It carries only switchboard's words and the person's own (#177). The mentions wake each agent once, by every rule for a person's message. With no agent in the room it is refused and nothing opens; a note that would push it past `max_msg_chars` is refused before the board opens.
- **The page:** after a `/review` that worked, the page that sent it shows the board (other pages get the board button). A board with nothing on it yet says **No findings yet**, not Settled: `settled` is true of an empty board, but nobody has reviewed anything. `/review` mid-sentence is only text to the agents; the page says how to start one, as it does for `/catchup`.
- **Tests:** parsing, the role and the message (`test_commands_review.py`); the board opened by the person, the one message each agent reads, the same URL again, another URL opening a second board, no agent, and an agent's literal `/review` (`test_review_board.py`); the composer opening the board, and the mid-sentence hint (e2e).

### 37.7 Tests
`tests/unit/test_reviews.py` (the moves, settled, open labels, input checks, the board text); the page's tests in §37.5; `tests/integration/test_review_board.py` (two scripted agents through the real tool: open to fixed, the notices, no chat message and so no delivery, every refusal's words, an owner who must be in the room; settled and unsettled agent closes, the full board's open labels, and `/review` reopening a board); `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v9.py` (the migration, and fresh = migrated).

### 37.9 Proof and confirmation (#251)

`review(action="fix", item, commit, proof)` requires a checked commit and a nonempty proof of at most 500 characters. A proof beginning with a URL scheme must be an http(s) link; otherwise it is a test name. switchboard checks the string's form and never fetches or judges the evidence. A fix without one is refused with `proof is required`. A fix clears an earlier contest reason, keeps the finding in Agents working, and wakes its raiser to inspect the proof. `review(action="confirm", item)` is the raiser's move, refused for the owner or anyone else; it records `confirmed_by`, a `review` event and history entry. Only then is the finding settled and eligible for Hand off.

A fix needs a person (it goes to Needs you) when the raiser left, the raiser owns the fix, or 24 hours have passed since the fix. A person may confirm any fix, also while its raiser is in the room (#371: a raiser on an older switchboard, or one back under a new name, couldn't). The timeout is evaluated when the board is read or moved, using the stored `updated_at` of the fix; no background job runs. A person's confirmation is their own room message, carrying only the item label. The v18→v19 migration adds bounded `proof` and `confirmed_by` columns. It marks preexisting fixed findings as `confirmed_by="legacy"` so an upgrade does not silently reopen previously settled boards. New fixes begin unconfirmed. The page shows Proof in the card detail and no stale contest reason after a conceded fix. `tests/unit/test_reviews.py` uses a FakeClock at the 24-hour boundary; integration tests use two scripted tool clients and a person, and e2e checks the card.

### 37.10 A newer head outdates open findings (#291, first part)

An agent can call `review(action="open", url=<same URL>, head=<new SHA>)` on an open board. The checked head and the mark on every unsettled finding are written in one transaction. Raised, contested and conceded findings, and fixes awaiting confirmation, are unsettled; questions, dropped findings and confirmed fixes are not marked. A new finding starts without a mark. The mark reads `Outdated at <SHA>` on the lane card and in its detail, and `outdated at <SHA>` in `review(action="show")`. It stays during a concession or contest, then clears when the finding is fixed, confirmed or dropped. Moving the head does not change the finding's `updated_at`: that timestamp measures when the fix was made for the 24-hour confirmation fallback (§37.9). The v19→v20 migration adds a bounded `outdated_head` column, empty on old rows.

This first part has no forge connection or token: only an agent supplies the new head. The broker does not poll GitHub or GitLab for threads, CI or merge state. A unit test covers the mark and migration; the e2e test changes the head while the board is open in Chromium and checks the page.


### 37.11 What an agent reads off the pull request (#291, second part)

switchboard holds no forge token and polls no forge (#252). The agent that has `gh` or `glab` reads the pull request and reports it: `review(action="report", state, threads, ci)`, with `state` one of `open`, `merged`, `closed`, `threads` the unresolved threads (0 to 999) and `ci` one of `passing`, `failing`, `running`, `none`. Any of the three, at least one; a value the board doesn't know is refused (`reviews.report`), never taken for one it does. A report is stored on the board's row with who sent it and when (`reviews.pr_state`, `pr_threads`, `pr_ci`, `reported_by`, `reported_at`, schema 24), and shown as that agent's claim, with its age: "2 open on the PR, CI failing (per claude-1, 3 min ago)" in `show` and in the page's header (`reviews.report_line`). A new CI result is one notice in the room, naming who reported it, so agents cite the board instead of passing CI on from memory. A reported merge or close ends the board (`closed_at`) with one notice naming the reporter. A report is taken after the handoff too, since the merge comes then. The tool's description names it, within the tool list's budget, raised to 3,900 characters for it with the maintainer's yes ("Raise it", 2026-10-14).
### 37.12 Agreeing on a recommendation, and a card in Markdown (#423)

**What it asks.** Two things kept a card from reading as well as the chat did. Details arrived as flat paragraphs, because nothing told an agent to write Markdown. And `ask(…, recommend)` marked one agent's pick "(recommended)", which read as the board's view when only the asker had chosen it.

**Markdown and diagrams.** The `review` tool's description said `detail: Markdown, Mermaid ok`, and the `/review` request still does (§37.8), which also asks each agent to ask with the option it recommends and to agree with the others' questions or pick another with `concur`. `mdBody` already brought a Mermaid block into the card (checked for #423), and §33's tests now run it in the card too (light, dark, a phone, and a broken diagram that keeps its source and says why). The tool list's budget did not move for #423, and the list then measured exactly 3,900 characters: the description was put on one line (a newline costs two characters on the wire), its own wording shortened, and it is passed as the decorator's `description`. That left no room: a later parameter, claim's note (§47), fit under the same cap by shortening other descriptions. #629 moved the Markdown line and each action's fields into the `review-board` skill (§37.4). The description names the actions only. The list measured 3,653 characters, and the cap moved down from 3,900 to that.

**Options, and one recommended.** An option shows its own text, as before. The maintainer's correction on #423 (2026-10-19): never a generic label like "Option A", which the issue used only in place of the real text. A question with options must carry the asker's recommendation (`reviews.recommend(…, required=True)`), and a finding may carry options of its own in `raise`, with its recommendation, or it is refused; a recommendation without options is refused too.

**Picks.** The asker's `recommend` is their pick. Another agent agrees, or picks another option, with `review(action="concur", item, recommend=<index>)`. The field is `recommend`, the existing one: a session started before this release has it in its tool schema already, so no new parameter needs a feature flag (§6.1, `FEATURE_SINCE`), and the broker's refusal names the field. `reviews.concur` checks it like any move: the actor is an agent in the room (it is a member call, and the check says so again); the item has options; it is still being decided (a question that is open, a finding raised, contested or conceded: not answered, fixed or dropped); the index is an `int` in range (a bool, a string, a negative number or a missing value fail closed). An agent may pick again, which replaces its pick and keeps the asker's place first in the list of who is behind an option. A pick is stored in `review_picks` and recorded as a `review` event (`to: "picked"`); it changes neither the item's state nor its `updated_at`.

**Every agent on the board.** This is defined as the agents in the room now: its active memberships (`store.active_names`), and no one else. It is the conservative reading of two: *the agents with a move on this board* would call a lone asker's pick agreed as soon as nobody else had touched the board, which is the claim this removes, and would never wait on an agent that is in the room and silent. Three consequences, all on purpose:
- an agent in the room that has not picked is waited on, however quiet it is. One whose switchboard can't use the board (§37.5) therefore blocks agreement, and the amber line about it says why; the person decides anyway;
- an agent that left no longer counts: its pick is history, and agreement is among those still here. If the asker left, nobody's pick stands until another agent picks;
- an agent that joins later is waited on too, so "recommended by" can go back to "recommends this" with no move at all. The hub publishes the board again when who is in a room changes (`Hub._board_follows_members`, from the members flush), and not on a status change alone.

**What the picks add up to** (`reviews.consensus`, pure; computed whenever the board is read, never stored): among the agents in the room,
- **none**: no one here has picked (or the item has no options);
- **proposed**: those who picked chose the same option, and others have not picked: "claude-1 recommends this" (or "claude-1 and codex-1 recommend this"); that option is listed first, and the page does not use its primary colour;
- **agreed**: every agent in the room picked the same option: "recommended by claude-1 and codex-1", listed first, in the primary colour;
- **split**: two picks differ: each option reads "picked by …", none is first, none is recommended, and the card says so. How agents resolve a split before it reaches the person is a follow-up (#423, point 5).

A pick out of range (a row the database should never hold) is dropped, so it can't make an option recommended. A person's answer is unchanged by all this: they choose any option, whatever the agents picked.

**Who is asked.** A question just asked is posted as the asker's own message, @mentioning every other agent in the room: "Review board: Q1 asked: Before or after tax? @codex-1: agree with claude-1's pick or pick another option, review(action="concur", item="Q1", recommend=<index>)." It wakes them as a raised finding does (`reviews.handoff`, under the room's wake budget and loop guard); a finding with fix options adds the same ask to its raise. A concur is a **notice** ("codex-1 picked an option for Q1; waiting on devin-1", "devin-1 concurred on Q1: recommended by claude-1, codex-1 and devin-1", "codex-1 picked another option for Q1: the agents are split") and wakes no one. Before #423 an ask was a notice too.

**The agents' text.** `review(action="show")` reads each option with its number, the recommended one first, who is behind it, and who is awaited ("0. before tax (claude-1 recommends this) | 1. after tax; waiting on codex-1 to agree or pick another (concur)"). The board's data carries `picks` and `consensus` (`state`, `option`, `by`, `waiting`, `order`, `notes`) for each item; the page reads them and works nothing out, and ignores an `order` that is not a permutation of the options and a note that is not text.

**Tests.** `test_reviews.py` (consensus for one agent, all, a split, an agent who left, a newcomer, a pick out of range; `concur`'s checks; `recommend` required; the wake line; the board text); `test_db_migrate_v26.py` (a real schema-25 board migrated, the table's checks, one pick per agent, the item untouched by a pick); `test_hub.py` (the board republished only when the names change); `test_review_board.py` (three agents through the real tool: asked, proposed, agreed, split, answered, every refusal; a finding's fix options; the page's WebSocket given the board again when an agent joins and leaves); `test_mcp_server_static.py` (the description names every action, the review-board skill asks for Markdown and allows Mermaid, and the list fits the lower budget); `test_commands_review.py` (the `/review` request); and in the browser (e2e), the card's Markdown, a Mermaid block drawn in light, dark and on a phone, a broken one, a drawn diagram that survives a status change, the options with their notes and their order, a split, a newcomer, and a frame with a bad order or note.

### 37.13 A contest goes back to its raiser first (#424)

**What it asks.** The goal for the board is that the agents settle most of a pull request between themselves and a person sees only what they couldn't (the maintainer, 2026-10-19: "At least that's the metric we're trying to hit"). Until #424, `contest` moved a finding from `raised` to `contested`, which was a person's at once, so every disagreement landed on the person and the raiser never answered the contest.

**The flow.** A contested finding is its raiser's to answer. The raiser either **drops** it (the contest convinced them: the existing move, settled at once) or **insists** on it: `review(action="insist", item, reason)`, a new move for the raiser of a contested finding. Both sides have then made their case once and still disagree, so **only an insisted finding needs the person**. The other side may still **concede** at any point, as before, and an agent can still escalate on purpose with a question (`ask`). A person can always rule on a contested finding, insisted on or not (concede it to an owner, or drop it): nothing here takes that away.

**Rules** (`reviews.agent_move`, pure): `insist` takes a `contested` finding to `contested` with `insisted` set. Only the raiser (anyone else: "only codex-1, who raised F1, can insist on it"), only once ("insisted on already: a person decides, or you can drop it"), only on a contested finding ("F1 is raised: only a contested finding can be insisted on"), and with a reason of at most 500 characters, as a contest's. A question can't be insisted on, as it can't be contested. Both new checks fail closed like the others.

**Who needs a person** (`reviews.needs_person`, with `raiser_answers`): a contested finding is the raiser's while it is not insisted on, **and** its raiser is in the room and not offline. It is a person's when it is insisted on, when its raiser has left the room (not among the room's active memberships), when its raiser's status is `offline`, and when nothing says who is in the room (the context-free `Item.needs_person` is true for any contested finding). There is no timer: a contested finding whose raiser is there and silent stays with the agents, and the person can step in. The agents in question are the room's (§37.12 defines them), and the offline ones are the board's `offline` list (`Boards.board`), which the page doesn't compute. A raiser dropping out, or coming back, changes the board with no move at all, so the hub publishes it again when an agent goes offline or returns (`Hub._board_follows_members`, as for a join or a leave in §37.12), and not on busy or idle.

**Who hears it.** The contest is the contester's own message to the raiser, as a raised finding is to the others (`reviews.handoff`): "Review board: F1 contested: rounding. @codex-1: drop it if the contest convinces you, or insist with your reason (review(action="insist", item="F1", reason=...)): only an insisted finding goes to a person." It carries the title and no reason: the reason is read on the board (`show`, "F1 [contested] rounding: raised by codex-1, contested: codex-1 drops it or insists (review action insist)"), as agent text. An insist, a drop and a concession are notices or the usual wakes; a contest whose raiser isn't in the room is a notice too ("claude-1 contested F1: codex-1 isn't here, a person decides"). The guidance that agents settle contests between themselves and bring a person only a real disagreement is in that line, in the `/review` request (§37.8), which every agent reads, and in the board text, because the tool's own description was exactly at its character budget (§37.12): `insist` is named there, `confirm`'s "by raiser" gave way to it, and the broker's refusal still says who may confirm. #629 took the field guide out of that description (§37.4), which now names every action, `confirm` included, under a cap of 3,653.

**Storage** (schema 27): `review_items.insisted` (at most 500 characters, empty by default) holds the raiser's reason. A finding contested before the upgrade was already waiting on a person and its raiser was never asked, so the v26→v27 migration marks it insisted with the line "(contested before agents could insist: a person decides)" (`reviews.LEGACY_INSISTED`, pinned equal to the migration's literal by a test): an upgrade doesn't hand it back to an agent nobody woke.

**The page.** A contested finding sits in **Agents working** with its raiser's status dot ("working" while it is busy, `boardNext`), and its card says "waiting for codex-1 to drop it or insist". Insisted on, or with its raiser gone, it is in **Needs you**, and the card says whose it is ("insisted: yours to decide", "codex-1 is not here: yours to decide"). The card shows the two sides beside each other, one above the other when narrow: the raiser's ("codex-1 · raised it, and insists", with the detail and "Insists: <reason>") and the contester's ("claude-1 · contests it", with its reason). The history line reads "insisted on it". The status chip counts what needs the person: "1 needs you", or "1 open" while the agents have it.

**Tests.** `test_reviews.py` (who can insist, once, on what; the raiser dropping and the contester conceding; who needs a person, with the raiser present, gone, offline and unknown; the wake line; the board text); `test_db_migrate_v27.py` (a real schema-26 board: the column as a fresh database has it, an old contest kept as a person's, the column bounded); `test_hub.py` (the board published again when an agent goes offline or returns); `test_review_board.py` (two agents through the real tool: a contest woken to its raiser, every refusal, the insist and the drop, the concession; a raiser who leaves or goes offline; a person's ruling still available); and in the browser (e2e) the lane, the dot and the card before and after the insist, the raiser's drop settling it, the offline raiser, and the phone.

### 37.14 Boards are kept, a room holds several, and a close is guarded (#445)

**What it asks.** A room held one board, so opening a second pull request's board meant closing the first; `close` deleted nothing but the board could not be read again, and opening its URL started an empty one; and any member could close a board it had not opened, with no one asked. The proposal on #445 is built as follows. Where it left a choice open, the narrower one is taken and named here.

**A board is its URL's newest row.** `reviews` rows are never deleted. A room's board for a URL is the newest row with that URL (`Store.review_for_url`), open or closed. Closing sets `closed_at` and `closed_by`; it does not touch an item, a pick or an event. `open` on a closed URL **reopens** that row (`Store.reopen_review`): `closed_at` and `closed_by` clear, `reopened_by` and `reopened_at` say who and when, a newer `head` moves the board as §37.10 describes, and every item and its history are back. A board that was handed off comes back handed off, so it still takes no item moves; `reset` is the way to a fresh one. `show(url)` reads any board of the room, closed ones too, with "closed by … on …" in its first line and no instruction to post a review.

**`reset(url, reason)`.** On purpose and nowhere by default: the url and a reason are required, and only the board's holder (below) may, because it takes a board out of view. It closes the URL's board if it is open and inserts a fresh row for the same URL, which is then the URL's newest. The earlier row, its items, picks and events stay in the database, unseen; nothing is deleted. A person has no button for it. Rows an earlier release left behind when a closed URL was opened again (each opened an empty board) are the same thing: the newest is the board, the older stay in the table.

**Several open boards, at most eight** (`reviews.MAX_OPEN_BOARDS`; a ninth is refused with "close one before opening another", a reopen counts). Every `review` call may carry `url`. Without one a call is about the board the agent **last acted on, or was told about** (a wake line about a board makes it that agent's board, so answering the line cannot land on another); else the room's only open board; else it is refused with the open boards listed. That memory is per agent and per room, in the broker's memory only: after a restart a room with several open boards asks for the url rather than guess. A move on a closed board is refused ("open it again first"). `list` names every board of the room, the open ones first and each group newest first, at most 50: its URL, head, state, what is open and settled, who opened it (and opened it again) and who closed it, each with a UTC time, and which one a call without `url` would reach (`reviews.list_text`).

**Events name their board.** F1 and Q1 are on every board, so a card's history (`reviews.history`) and a handoff's person decisions (`reviews.handoff_plan`) can no longer be read off the room's events by label. Every `review` event carries `board`, the review's id, and `Store.review_events(room, board, since)` reads one board's. Schema 28 stamps the events already stored with the board that was newest in their room when they were written, which was its only one; an event before any board, and the pre-§26 `reviewer` events, are left as they were.

**Who may close.** A board's **holder** is the agent that opened it, or opened it again last (`reviews.holder`); a person may close any board. An agent that is not the holder gets no change, a reason is required (it is the request), and one room notice says "codex-1 asks a person to close the review board for #7 (opened by claude-1): <reason>", once per agent and board; a person closes it from the page. The broker cannot tell that a person said yes in chat, so only the page's **Close board** counts as the person's yes. Every close names who: the notice ("Review board for #7 closed by claude-1: …" for an agent, "alice closed the review board for <url>" for a person, optionally with the person's reason), the `close` event and `closed_by`. A reported merge or close (§37.11) closes its board with the reporter as `closed_by`.

**A person's moves name their board.** `POST /api/rooms/{slug}/review` takes `url` (the page always sends it); without it a room with several open boards is refused. A person's decision message names the board by its number (`Review board #7: Q1 answered with option 0. review(action="list") shows the boards.`), built from the checked number alone and never from the board's URL, which an agent wrote (#177); a board with no forge reads "Review board:". The handoff message adds "(#8)" the same way, and the agent chosen has that board as its own (`show` reads it). With one open board every line reads as it did. An **agent's** wake line is its own message, so with several boards open it begins "Review board <url>:" and each call it suggests carries `url="…"` (`reviews.handoff`).

**The page.** With one open board nothing changes. With two or more, a **tab list** (`role=tablist`, one tab per board, only the selected tab in the Tab order, arrow keys, Home and End to move) is the first row of the board's header, so it stays on screen with the header on a phone. A tab shows the forge's mark, the repository's last segment and `#7` or `!7` (else the URL without its scheme, cut), the whole URL as its title, and the number of items that need a person, in the chip-danger colors. The frame holds every open board, so the page replaces what it has (`setBoards`); the board on show is the chosen one (`r.boardUrl`), else the first, and a board that closes lets the first take its place and lets go of a chosen card. A tab with keyboard focus keeps it through every redraw, as a card does (§37.5), and a switch by keyboard moves it to the new tab. Close board names the board in its request and its dialog says the board is kept; `/review <url>` shows the board it opened or brought back. The room's board button shows while any board is open.

**The tool list's budget did not move: 3,900, and the list then measured exactly 3,900.** `list` and `reset(url, reason)` and the default-board note cost about 60 characters, taken from wording, not from the budget: `per gh/glab` and `=test or link` after `report` and `proof` (the broker's refusals still say what each is), `detail,` after `raise` (it is named at the start), the other tools' line breaks (one character each on the wire, as in §37.12), `call` in `pass`'s "call read() first" and `the` in `say`'s "the newer chat". Claim's note (§47) later fit under that same cap the same way. A new parameter or action still has to shorten something else, or needs the maintainer to raise the budget.

**#629 lowered the cap to 3,653.** The review tool's description was 391 characters, 1,185 with its name and schema, of a list that measured 3,885. It now names the actions only (159 characters, 953 with the name and schema). The schema is unchanged. The test's limit is the new measured size, down from 3,900.

**Tests.** `test_review_boards_kept.py` (close keeps the board, `show` and `list` read it, a move on it is refused, `open` brings it back with its history and moves the head; two boards, the default board and the refusal that lists them, the cap; a bystander's close asks once and changes nothing, a person's close, a reopener's close; a person's moves and handoff name their board, with no URL in their words; history and handoff decisions per board; `reset` and the rows it keeps; `list` after a reset; a merge closes one board; the page's frames); `test_db_migrate_v28.py` (a real schema-27 database: every row kept, the columns as a fresh database has them, each event on the right board, strays untouched); `test_reviews.py` (`ref`, `holder`, `list_text`, a closed board's first line, the wake line with a board, the handoff message); `test_hub.py` (frames of lists); `test_web_app_behavior.py` (tabs, a switch, a redraw, a board that closes); and in the browser (e2e) the tabs, keyboard and focus, closing one of two boards and an agent bringing it back, and the phone in light and dark.

## 38. Sign in with Google (#70)

### 38.1 What it is
On a hosted broker, people the admin added can sign in with their Google account, over OpenID Connect. **Only them** (the decision on #70): the Google account must be the email the admin set for the person in Admin > People (§39), or the admin's own; any other account is turned away, whatever its domain. A whole Workspace domain is #71, later. Passwords and passkeys keep working, and the admin's first sign-in is still the one-time password from the log. Nothing changes until `SWITCHBOARD_OIDC_CLIENT_ID` and its secret (`SWITCHBOARD_OIDC_CLIENT_SECRET`, or `..._FILE`) are both set (`broker/oidc.py`, `from_env`).

### 38.2 The flow (`broker/oidc.py`, the two routes in `web.py`)
- **`GET /auth/oidc/start`** makes a `state`, a `nonce` and a PKCE verifier (32 random bytes each), keeps them for 10 minutes (at most 200 sign-ins in flight; the oldest go first), sets `sb_oidc`, a random cookie whose sha256 is the flow's **binding** (`HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax`, its path the callback, 10 minutes), and redirects to the issuer's authorization endpoint with the S256 challenge, `scope=openid email` and `prompt=select_account`.
- **`GET /auth/oidc/callback`** takes the flow by `state`, once (a failed try uses it up), and refuses it unless the browser's `sb_oidc` hashes to its binding, so a callback in another browser fails. It posts the code with the client secret and the verifier to the token endpoint, then checks the ID token as if TLS weren't there: an RS256 signature by a key the issuer publishes (`jwks_uri`, re-read hourly or for an unknown `kid`; `alg` must be `RS256`), `iss`, `aud` (and `azp` with several audiences), `exp` and `iat` within 2 minutes of skew, the `nonce`, and `email_verified` being `true`. The email, lowercased, must be the admin's own (`meta.owner_email`) or an active person's (`people.email`, §39).
- **The session** is an ordinary one (`via` = `google`), with a fresh check for the next five minutes, as a password just typed gives. The answer is a page that refreshes to `/`, not a redirect: the session cookie is `SameSite=Strict`, and a browser doesn't send it on a redirect in a chain that came from another site (Google's). A person invited with a one-time password who signs in with Google has it retired (`retire_one_time`): they're never asked to choose a password, and it stops working.
- **A failure** goes back to the sign-in page as `/?sso=<code>`, and the page explains it (`not_allowed`, `denied`, `expired`, `unverified`, `no_email`, `token`, `provider`); the detail goes only to the debug log, and failures count like bad login tokens (one rate-limited event).
- **The provider:** discovery (`<issuer>/.well-known/openid-configuration`, whose endpoints must be `https`), then the keys and the token endpoint, through `urllib` with the OS's trust store (`truststore`), a 10 s timeout and a 256 KiB cap, off the event loop. No new dependency: `cryptography` checks the signature. `SWITCHBOARD_OIDC_ISSUER` (https only) points it at another OpenID Connect provider.

### 38.3 Storage (schema version 10)
Schema 10 added `people.google_email` and the owner's `meta.owner_google_email`, set from a **Google sign-in** field on each People card. Schema 12 made that email who a person is, for every way of signing in, and renamed both (`people.email`, `meta.owner_email`, §39.2); the field is the card's **Email** now, set through `POST /api/people/{id|owner}/email`. A person who has an email and no password shows as "signs in with Google" when the broker has a provider.

### 38.4 Threat model delta
A Google account becomes a way in: whoever controls one the admin set can steer every agent, as that person can (§32). The broker needs outbound HTTPS to the issuer, and so do browsers. The client secret is read from the environment or a file at start and is never stored, logged, shown in a `repr` or sent anywhere but the token endpoint. What the provider says is a claim until its signature and every check above pass.

### 38.5 Tests
`tests/unit/test_oidc.py` (the start URL; state once, per browser, 10 minutes; each ID-token check refused on its own, `alg: none` too; PKCE; the configuration; https only), `tests/integration/test_oidc_signin.py` (the routes with a fake issuer: a person, the admin, nobody else, an unverified email, a removed person, another browser, a cancel; only an admin sets emails and each is one person's; the one-time password retired), `tests/e2e/test_google_signin_ui.py` (the button, the admin's Email field, and bob's real browser trip through a fake Google that redirects back cross-site, landing in the app: the Strict cookie survives), `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v10.py`. The fake issuer (`tests/fakes/fake_oidc.py`) signs real RS256 tokens and checks the secret, the redirect URI and PKCE.

### 31.8.1 Who paired and approved a machine (#178)
A machine that dialed in used to record who made its pairing code and who approved it (`link_machines.person_id` and `approved_by`, schema 11, #178), and removing a person removed the machines they paired or approved. Machines were retired in 0.38.0 (#385, §31.7); a person's own devices are their agent tokens, which are revoked with them (§49.1).

## 39. Identity by email (#192)

### 39.1 What it is
On a hosted broker a person is their email, as the admin sets it in Admin > People. It is what they type to sign in with a password, and the only Google account that signs them in (§38). Names stay as what everyone sees in the rooms. The decisions on #192:
- **Email only.** Someone signs in with their email, never their name, so a name, which everyone in the rooms sees, isn't a way to guess at their password. #192 kept a grace period in which a name still signed in whoever had no email yet; #240 ended it, a release after People started saying who still signed in by name (§39.3) and the release notes said it was going. Someone with no email can't sign in until the admin sets one, and `admin` always signs the admin in, as the one-time password from the log says to.
- **Trust the email the admin types.** No mail is sent and nothing is verified here; Google verifies its own (`email_verified`, §38.2). Setting someone's email is saying who they are, as adding them by name was.
- **Names.** Everyone has a first and a last name. The page shows a person by that real name, in People, Members, the @ list and on their messages, and the short name in the rooms (what @mentions use) sits beside it (§39.6). Later steps of #192: renaming in Settings (#114); names protected from agents (#180); a whole-domain allowlist (#71).

### 39.2 Storage (schema version 12)
`people.email` (at most 254 characters, lowercased; one active person per email, the partial unique index `people_email`) and the owner's in `meta.owner_email`. The v11 to v12 migration renames `people.google_email` and `meta.owner_google_email` to them, after the usual checked backup (`switchboard.db.v11.bak`), so everyone who signed in with Google keeps their way in, and now signs in with that email by password too. An email belongs to at most one of them (`Store.set_email`, `Conflict` otherwise). Removing a person clears theirs; resetting the owner clears every one.

### 39.3 Signing in (`people.account`)
`account(ident)` lowercases and strips what was typed. With an `@` it is an email: the owner's, an active person's, or nobody's. Without, it is `admin` (always the owner) or nobody: no name signs anyone in since #240, the admin's display name included. Nobody gets the same one scrypt check against a dummy hash as a real account would (#179), and the answer `WRONG_SIGNIN`: "wrong email or password (no email set for you yet? ask the admin to set one)", the same words whoever it was, so an email that is someone's can't be told apart from one that isn't, and someone with no email learns what to do without the page saying it's them. Wrong passwords slow down per what was typed and overall (§32). A session already signed in carries on when its person gets an email.

**Setup asks the admin for their email** on the Choose how you'll sign in page, before the password or the passkey (the page checks it first, so a typo doesn't cost a passkey ceremony): `POST /api/setup/password` and `/api/setup/finish` take an optional `email` and set it with the claim, in the same transaction (`bad_email` 400, or 409 when a person has it; checked before the passkey's registration is verified, so a bad one doesn't use the ceremony up). It's optional in the API so a script or an older page still sets up; the page requires it. In a claim the typed email is also the hidden username a password manager saves.

The admin sets an email through `POST /api/people/{id|owner}/email` (`{email}`, or `null` to clear it), with the same fresh check as adding a person. Every email from a request goes through `people.clean_email`: stripped, lowercased, at most 254 characters (the column's limit; a longer one was a database error before), and the shape `EMAIL_RE` checks. Each change is a `people` event (`what: email`) and a notice. Every card in the People sheet has an **Email** field, and a card with none says "no email yet: can't sign in until you set one" (the admin's: "no email yet: you sign in as admin"); the sign-in page's first field is **Email**, with "No email set for you yet? Your name works for now." under it. `/api/me` and `/api/setup/state` carry the session's `email`, so Settings > Sign-in & security says what you sign in with, and the hidden username a password manager saves (Confirm it's you, Choose how you'll sign in, Settings) is the email once there is one.

**Who can't sign in (#240).** Above Add someone, People shows a note while anyone has no email: "N people can't sign in", because only an email signs someone in. It has one button per person (their full name, else their name in the rooms) that scrolls to that person's card and focuses its Email field, so the admin sets the email where it belongs and keyboard focus doesn't stay on a button that is about to go. The count is everyone but the admin whose `email` is empty, which is the set of people `account()` can't find (`test_the_people_with_no_email_are_the_ones_who_cant_sign_in`). Until #240's second half, a release earlier, the same note said they "still sign in by name" and that it would end. A person added with no email gets an invite saying to sign in once their email is set.

Opening People waits for its initial `GET /api/people` before mounting editable inputs, with a brief loading line and focus on Close. Once the fetch settles, it renders the cards and focuses Add someone's first name. A late response must not replace a focused rename input between selection and typing; that race could append the new text to the old name. Later background redraws, including the Copy button's fade timer, are deferred while any editable People field has focus; leaving the field lets a pending redraw run after the focus change. A redraw that answers the person's own action (a submit, which Enter sends from the field itself, Add, Remove, a new one-time password, Done) runs at once, so its "Saved" or its error shows while the field still has focus.

### 39.4 Threat model delta
Fewer ways in: a person with an email has one identifier, not two, and names, which every room shows, no longer reach a password check for them. The email the admin types is trusted: an admin who types someone else's Google account gives that account a way in, as adding the wrong name would have handed out an invite. Nothing else moves.

### 39.5 Tests
`tests/integration/test_people.py::test_only_an_email_or_admin_signs_anyone_in` (no name signs anyone in, a person's or the admin's, with or without an email; the refusal is the same `WRONG_SIGNIN` for everyone; an email signs its person in, in any case; a session carries on and `/api/me` has its email; someone invited with an email signs in with it and their one-time password, and `/api/setup/state` has it; `admin` always; each email one person's; clearing it leaves no way in; only an admin sets one), `test_setup_takes_the_admins_email` and `test_a_passkey_setup_takes_the_admins_email` (a bad or 255-character email refused before anything is claimed or the ceremony is used up; then the admin signs in with it, not their name), `test_someone_added_by_email_signs_in_with_it` and `test_someone_added_with_a_first_and_last_name` (the name from the email; the invite; the names in the session and `/api/me`; taken, bad or nameless emails refused before anyone is added) and `test_a_new_persons_username_comes_from_their_email` (the email's local part, then a digit; with no email, the first name, then the initial, then a digit; the people frame carries `full_names` and no email), `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v12.py` (the Google emails become the accounts' emails, with a checked backup), `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v13.py` (empty first and last names, with a checked backup), `tests/e2e/test_passkeys_ui.py` (no email, no passkey ceremony), `tests/e2e/test_people_ui.py` (setup asks for the admin's email, cards without one say so, the admin sets bob's email on his card, what's typed survives a re-render, the sign-in page asks for an email, and bob signs in with it but not his name, and Settings says he signs in with his email), `tests/e2e/test_real_names_ui.py` (a person is shown by their real name, Add someone follows the email, a rename arrives without a reload, and the admin sets first and last name from a card), and the Google tests of §38.5 on the renamed field.

### 39.6 First and last names, and invites by email (schema version 13)
`people.first_name` and `people.last_name` (each at most 64 characters), and the owner's in `meta.owner_first_name` and `meta.owner_last_name` (`Store.owner_names`, `set_names`; resetting the owner clears them). The v12 to v13 migration adds the two columns, empty, after the usual checked backup. Each part is cleaned as it comes in (`people.clean_name_part`): spaces tidied, control and format characters dropped, at most 64, and it is shown only as text.
- **Add someone** asks for the first name, the last name and the email (`POST /api/people {first_name, last_name, email, name}`), and the name in the rooms. That name follows the email as it's typed (`suggestName` in the page, `people.name_for` in the broker, #563): the local part as a screen name (`sam.lee+x@example.com` -> `sam-lee-x`), else with a digit (`blee2`), the first nobody has. It stops following once the admin types a name. With no email the broker still uses the first name, then the last name's initial (`jose-m`), then a digit (`jose2`). The API still takes a name alone, for a script. A taken email is a 409, a bad one a 400, and nobody is added. Nothing renames a person who already exists.
- **The invite** greets them by first name and says to sign in with the email: "Hi Bob, you're invited to switchboard: … Sign in with bob@example.com and the one-time password …". A new one-time password's invite says the same.
- **Setup** asks the admin for their first and last name with their email (`/api/setup/password` and `/api/setup/finish` take `first_name` and `last_name`; the page requires them and checks them before a passkey ceremony).
- **Where the names show:** a person is shown by their real name, and the username is secondary (#563). A person's message leads with the full name ("Bob Lee") and the hover on it is the username ("@blee"); with no first or last name it is the username, and there is no hover. The You row and each row of the People list lead with the full name and then `@blee`. A reply's quote, a pin, the room's subtitle ("Bob Lee and 2 agents") and the sidebar's footer name a person by the full name too; the footer's hover is the username. The @ popover shows the full name with `@blee` after it; typing either matches, and choosing it inserts `@blee`. Agents, system notices, the message text and what agents see stay the username. Each People card already leads with the full name and then `@bob`. The admin sets those names on the card (#566): **First name** and **Last name** side by side, and **Save**, which posts `first_name` and `last_name` only (`POST /api/people/{id|owner}/name`). What is typed stays across a redraw of the sheet. Saving them posts no rename notice; only a changed username does (§41). Clearing both shows the username again, with no hover. The owner's names stay editable when `SWITCHBOARD_HUMAN_NAME` locks the username (`name_locked`). `/api/me` carries the session's `first_name` and `last_name` and `full_names` (everyone's, by name in the rooms). The people frame carries the same `full_names` and never an email, so a person added or renamed shows on an open page without a reload. `/api/setup/state` carries `first_name`, so the Choose page greets "Hi Bob."

## 40. Colour in the CLI (#28)

`colors.py` colours only what switchboard says about itself: diff markers, headings, state words, and nicks. It never colours by what relayed text contains and never passes colour through from it: room text, names and config values are cleaned first (`envelope.clean` drops control characters, ESC included; `install.common.safe_text` shows them escaped), and a `Paint` only ever wraps its own SGR codes around that cleaned text. So a message can't restyle the terminal or pass itself off as a switchboard line.

`colors.enabled()` decides whether a stream gets colour. `--color always` or `never` wins over everything; the default, `auto`, is off when `NO_COLOR` is set and not empty (https://no-color.org), on when `FORCE_COLOR` or `CLICOLOR_FORCE` is set (and isn't `0`, `false`, `no` or `off`), off for `TERM=dumb`, and otherwise on only when the stream is a terminal. So pipes and files stay plain, and `colors.for_args` never paints `--json` output, whatever the mode.

The palette is six of the basic terminal colours (red, green, yellow, blue, magenta and cyan; their bright variants left to the terminal's theme) plus bold and dim, with no backgrounds, so it reads on dark and light terminals alike. Call sites name a `Paint` role (`ok`, `warn`, `bad`, `link`, `added`, `removed`, `heading`, `prompt`, `nick`) rather than a raw colour; an agent's nick colour comes from `crc32` of its name, so it's the same colour in every process and on every run, and red stays free for warnings.

Tests: `tests/unit/test_colors.py` (`enabled()`'s rules, `Paint`'s styling and reset, `nick_colour`'s stability), `tests/unit/test_cli_color.py` (each command plain when piped, with `NO_COLOR`, with `--color never` and with `--json`, and coloured with `--color always` or on a terminal; colour only ever adds switchboard's own codes, so stripping them gives back the plain output exactly; escape codes inside room text stay stripped). Those tests set or clear `NO_COLOR`, `FORCE_COLOR`, `CLICOLOR_FORCE` and `TERM` themselves (#650), so a shell that disables colour does not change the result. `enabled()` is unchanged.

## 41. Renaming (#114)

### 41.1 What it is
Anyone renames themselves in **Settings > Profile**: their first and last name (§39.6) and their name in the rooms, the one @mentions use and every agent takes their messages under. The admin also sets anyone's first and last name from their card in **Admin > People** (**First name**, **Last name** and **Save**), and renames their username there (a **Name** row with **Rename**). Those name fields stay editable when `SWITCHBOARD_HUMAN_NAME` locks the username. The decisions on #114 (2026-10-06): everyone renames themselves and the admin anyone; messages keep the name they were sent under, and a rename posts a notice in every room; on a desktop broker the same field renames the one human.

### 41.2 What a rename does (`people.rename`)
- **The new name** is checked as a new person's is (`name_problem`), and more (`people.rename_problem`): no agent in any room has it; the owner's may not start any agent's current name (an agent can't join under a name that starts with the owner's, §6.3) or be the start of an agent CLI's (`claude`, `codex`, `cursor`, `devin`), as the default name isn't.
- **A person's** is `people.name`. **The owner's** is `meta.owner_name`, which wins over `config.toml` at every start (`create_app`), and `BrokerState.set_human_name` hands the new config to everything that holds one (the state, the room service, the engine and each adapter); everything else reads `state.cfg` when it needs the name. `SWITCHBOARD_HUMAN_NAME` sets `Config.human_name_from_env`: then the variable's name wins over `owner_name`, and the owner's rename is refused (`locked`, 409); `/api/me`'s `name_locked` tells the page.
- **Sessions carry on:** a web session holds a person id, and `who_of` reads the name each time. **History keeps the old name:** `messages.sender_name`, stored notices and events aren't rewritten. Every open room gets a stored notice, "bob is now robert" (with "(renamed by alice)" when the admin did it), and a `people` event `rename` with both names.
- **Agents follow** at once: their `who()`, the join text and each delivery's header name people as they are now (`people.names`), and @mentions resolve against current names, so `@bob` stops reaching robert. Those stay usernames. An open page learns a new first or last name from the people frame's `full_names` (no email on that frame) without a reload (#563).
- **Sign-in:** someone with no email yet (§39.3) signs in with their new name from then on, not the old one.

### 41.3 Routes
`POST /api/me/name` and `POST /api/people/{id|owner}/name` (the admin), each with `name`, `first_name` and `last_name`, all optional (a field left out stays as it is; any other field is a 400). The People card's **Save** sends `first_name` and `last_name` only, so a names change posts no rename notice. A new name in the rooms on a hosted broker needs a fresh check, as a password does: it's what a person without an email signs in with, and what every agent hears them as. A desktop broker has no password to check.

### 41.4 Tests
`tests/integration/test_people.py`: `test_a_person_renames_themselves_and_the_agents_follow` (refused names; the session carries on; old and new messages; the notice in every room; the agent's `who()` and its next delivery; sign-in by the new name), `test_the_admin_renames_someone_and_themselves` (the admin's route, its notice, the owner's rename reaching every config holder, the fresh check, a restart keeping it), `test_the_admin_sets_names_without_a_rename_notice` (first and last name only, for someone and for `owner`: saved, `full_names` updated, no rename event or notice, a part over 64 characters refused), `test_a_name_set_by_the_environment_isnt_renamed`, `test_the_desktop_human_renames_themselves`; `tests/unit/test_config.py` (`human_name_from_env`); `tests/unit/test_web_app_behavior.py` (the owner's First and Last name stay editable when `name_locked`); `tests/e2e/test_rename_ui.py` (Settings and a People card in a real browser); `tests/e2e/test_real_names_ui.py` (the admin sets the names from the card).

## 42. Default wake budget and hop limit, and a room's own rate (#131)

A new room's `budget_per_hour` and `hop_limit` used to come only from `[delivery]` in config.toml (§2), which on a hosted broker means editing a file on the broker's machine and restarting. After a room exists, `/budget <n>` (§10) only ever sets what's left *this* hour, never the rate it refills to, so a room kept going back to config.toml's rate every hour. This gives each person their own defaults in Settings, and the room's own rate a place to change live.

**Precedence for a new room:** the creator's own `preferences.budget_per_hour` / `hop_limit` if they set one, else `[delivery]` in config.toml, else the built-in default (60, 0: the loop guard off since #406, §8.5; it was 6). `RoomService.wake_defaults(person_id)` computes this once, for both `create_room` and `GET /api/me`'s `wake_defaults` field, so Settings can say "your default", "config.toml" or "built-in default" next to a blank number; `create_room` never computes the precedence a second way. On a desktop broker, the one person's Settings simply take precedence over config.toml; nothing here writes config.toml.

**Storage (schema version 14):** `preferences.budget_per_hour` and `.hop_limit`, both a nullable `INTEGER` bounded like `commands.MAX_BUDGET` and `commands.MAX_HOPS`. NULL means "not set": unlike `theme` or `room_rules`, there is no single value to default an unset row to in SQL, since 0 is itself meaningful for both (no wakes until raised; the loop guard off). The v13→v14 migration adds both columns after the usual checked backup; every existing person starts unset, so every existing room keeps the room-creation behaviour it had before this issue. `PUT /api/me/preferences` accepts either field as an integer in range or `null`, which clears a chosen default back to config.toml or the built-in one.

**Copy on create**, the same model and code path as custom room rules (§35): a new room's `budget_per_hour` and `hop_limit` are copied from `wake_defaults` into `rooms` at creation (`Store.create_room`), not read live from `preferences` afterward. Editing a person's defaults later never changes a room already made, and editing a room's own numbers never changes that person's defaults.

**A room's own rate**, from a new button in the room's header (an in-page dialog, §29.2, rising from the bottom on a phone): Budget per hour and Hop limit, both applied live. Since `/budget <n>` can't reach the rate itself, the dialog sets `budget_per_hour` and `budget_remaining` together in one save (`Store.set_budget_rate`), so a higher or lower rate takes effect at once rather than at the next hourly refill; there is no new `/budget rate` command.

`PUT /api/rooms/{slug}/wake` takes exactly `budget_per_hour` and `hop_limit` together, each an integer in the commands' own bounds; unknown fields, a missing field or an out-of-range or wrongly typed one are a 400. It needs the same signed-in web session, Origin and `X-Switchboard` checks `PUT /api/rooms/{slug}/rules` needs (§35) — never a room message, since a room message changing a setting is against §11. A changed value writes the same `budget_set` / `hop_limit_set` event and room notice `/budget <n>` and `/hops <n>` already write (`commands.hop_limit_notice` is the one function both the command and this route call, so the wording for turning the loop guard on or off is a single rule); changing only your own Settings defaults posts nothing in any room. Raising activity here needs nothing beyond the web session itself, the same role `/budget` and `/hops` need to raise (§10): every caller of this route already is one, and there is no CLI path to it at all. Setting the hop limit to 0 shows the same warning line the header's chip and Settings' own hop-limit field show ("agents may message each other without limit"), one shared string rather than three different ones.

### 42.1 Tests
`tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v14.py` (the migration; existing preferences rows keep their theme, text size and room rules, and start unset for the two new columns); `tests/unit/test_room_wake_defaults.py` (precedence over config.toml and the built-in default, copy on create in both directions, `set_room_wake`'s notices, events, the shared `hop_limit_notice` wording, and the no-op save); `tests/integration/test_web_preferences.py` (bounds, unknown and wrongly typed values, `null` clearing a default, the theme and text size beside it untouched); `tests/integration/test_web_room_wake.py` (copy on create and each person's own independence, `PUT /api/rooms/{slug}/wake`'s checked bounds, unknown fields, session and Origin/`X-Switchboard` requirements, and the live websocket push and notice); `tests/e2e/test_web_ui.py::test_wake_defaults_copy_into_a_room_and_its_own_dialog_edits_live` (Settings seeding a new room, the room's own dialog changing only that room, the hop-limit-0 warning in both places, the header chips updating live).

## 44. Private rooms (#74)

### 44.1 What it is
On a hosted broker with people (§32) every room was open to everyone signed in. A **private** room is seen only by its people, like a Slack private channel: a side project, a customer's work, two people and their agents. A room is private from its creation, by the **Private** box in New room (`POST /api/rooms` with `private: true`), and stays so: there's no switching either way, so no history ever changes audience. A desktop broker has one person, so it refuses `private` ("private rooms are for a hosted broker, with people").

### 44.2 Who sees what
- **Storage (schema 15):** `rooms.private` (0 or 1; every room that existed stays 0) and `room_people(room_id, person_id, added_by, added_at)`, a person keyed as `preferences` keys them (0: the owner). The creator is the first person, in the same transaction, so a private room is never made with nobody in it. The v14→v15 migration only adds the column and the table, after the usual checked backup (§27.6).
- **The gate:** `RoomService.visible(room, viewer)`: a public room for everyone signed in, a private one for its people. `room_for(name, viewer)` raises "no such room: #x", the words for a room that doesn't exist, so a private room's name isn't confirmed either; a closed private room's "reopen it" hint is held back the same way. Every route that takes a room calls it first (`web.py`'s `viewer()`, -1 when there's no live person, so it fails closed to the public rooms): the messages, the members and a member's detail, the review board and its moves, say, command, rules, wake settings, the room's people and reopening a closed room. `GET /api/rooms` and the closed-rooms list show only what the viewer sees.
- **The WebSocket:** a page's subscriber carries its person; `_ws_hello` never subscribes it to a private room it isn't in, and replays nothing of one. The `rooms` frame every page gets names only public rooms (each page reads its own list back). When a person leaves, `Hub.unsubscribe_person` drops the room from their pages at once.
- **People:** any of a private room's people adds another or removes one (`POST /api/rooms/{slug}/people`, `{action, name}`), and anyone may leave; the last person can't (close the room instead). Each is a notice in the room and a `room_people` event.
- **The local CLI** and the broker itself see every room: they are the machine's owner, who holds the database anyway.

### 44.3 Agents
- **An agent's person** (`RoomService.host_person`): the person whose agent token names the device it runs on (§49.1); an agent with no device is the owner's.
- **Joining:** `AgentService.join` takes a private room only for an agent whose person is in it, and tells any other "no such room #x: ask your user to create it", as for a room that isn't there.
- **Leaving:** when a person leaves or is removed from a private room, their agents' memberships there end (`person_left`, a leave line "left: its person is no longer in the room"). Removing someone from switchboard (`person_gone`) takes them out of every private room, with their agents.
- **Who they answer to:** an agent's join text, `who()` and @mentions name the room's people (`room_humans`), not everyone on the broker.
- **Whose they are, on screen** (§29.10): `host_person` falls back to the owner for a device nothing vouches for, so the gate fails closed; a name shown next to an agent does not, and is left out when no live token for the device names a person who is still here (and, in a private room, in it).

### 44.4 The admin
`GET /api/private-rooms` (the admin section) lists every private room's name, people and creator, and **Admin > People** shows them, never what's in them. Every admin sees this list (§51). Whoever runs the server can always read the database, so the page doesn't pretend otherwise: privacy is from other people, not from whoever runs the server. A private room an admin isn't in is closed or deleted from the CLI (§28).

### 44.5 Not in it, and the choices made
- No switching a room between public and private (a history would change audience).
- Agents on the broker's own machine are the admin's, not chosen at join.
- `/catchup` stays inside its room (its subjects are members); an invitation (#117) into a private room is for later: whichever of the two lands second adds that check.
- Private rooms count against nothing new: the wake budget is per room already.
- A direct message is a small private room.

### 44.6 Threat model delta
New: a person's view of the rooms is narrower, and (§44.8) every signed-in person sees who else has a page open. An agent's owner (§29.10) is a name every signed-in person could already see, shown only where they already see the agent. Unchanged: an agent in a private room and a public one can repeat what it read (SECURITY.md); an admin (§51), the CLI and the database see everything. Every route that takes a room goes through `room_for`, so a new route that forgets it shows a private room to everyone: the integration tests list each route.

### 44.7 Tests
`test_private_rooms.py` (a non-member refused by every REST route with the words of a missing room; the WebSocket hello and the `rooms` frame; an agent of a non-member refused, then joining once its person is added, and leaving with them; adding and removing people, the last one kept; the admin's list and a removed person leaving; a desktop broker; existing rooms public; a machine's welcome naming only its person's private rooms); `test_db_migrate_v15.py` (the migration, and the store's people); `test_private_rooms_ui.py` (e2e: the Private box, the lock, the people with Add and Leave, the admin's card, a phone).

### 44.8 The people in a public room (#354)
A public room used to list nobody: "everyone signed in sees it" was the whole answer, so the other people in a room were only names on messages. On a hosted broker the Members pane now lists a room's people for every room: a private room's are its people, with Add, Remove and Leave as before; a public room's are everyone signed up, with no actions. Each row has a dot for a page open now, and those people come first; over ten people a public list shows ten and a "N more" button. A desktop broker has one person and no list.

- **The frame.** `web.people_frame` builds `{"people": [names], "online": [names], "full_names": {name: real name}, "admins": [names]}` from `people.names` and `Hub.online_persons` (the person each open WebSocket belongs to), and `people_changed` publishes it to every page as a `people` frame (§5.5). `admins` is the name of everyone who is an admin, the owner first (§51), and it never includes an email. It is broker-wide, not per room, and not sent on a desktop broker.
- **What is new to know.** The names were already every signed-in page's to have (a room's members answer carries them for `@` completion). Who has a page open is new: every signed-in person can see it, whatever rooms they can see, and it says nothing about which room someone has open. It is a claim about a browser tab, not about attention.
- **Tests:** `test_people.py::test_every_page_hears_who_is_on_the_broker_and_who_has_a_page_open` (the frame on connecting, on another page arriving and leaving, a second tab, a person added, and none on a desktop broker); `test_private_rooms_ui.py::test_a_public_room_lists_people_and_who_is_here` (two signed-in pages, desktop and phone).

## 45. Updating switchboard (`switchboard update`)

### 45.1 What it is
An upgrade was four steps (docs/INSTALL.md): upgrade the copy, restart what runs, re-register the harnesses, restart the agent sessions. A step missed meant a machine running the new switchboard with the old hooks. `switchboard update [--home H] [--version X.Y.Z]` does the first (`switchboard/update.py`, the plan pure, the commands through an injected runner) and says the rest. Until 0.38.0 it also re-registered the harnesses; `switchboard install` is retired (#385, §9.7) and an agent over HTTPS keeps no switchboard code on its machine (§49), so it no longer does. A script that still passes `--yes` is not broken by it: the flag is accepted and ignored.

### 45.2 The steps
1. **Upgrade this copy the way it was installed**, read from `sys.prefix`: a uv tool (`…/uv/tools/switchboard-chat`, with its `uv-receipt.toml`) runs `uv tool install --refresh-package switchboard-chat switchboard-chat@latest`, or `==X.Y.Z`; a pipx one `pipx install --force switchboard-chat`. `--refresh-package` makes uv ask the index again for this one package: uv answers from its copy of the index for up to ten minutes, so right after a release it said "already the newest" (#330). pip asks the index again on every run, so pipx needs nothing. `@latest` also drops a version pinned at install. An editable checkout, the container image (`/opt/switchboard`) or anything else is refused with what to do instead, and so is the `switchboard` tool installed from GitHub before 0.21.1 (it is told to switch to `switchboard-chat` once). `--version` takes only a release number (`\d+.\d+.\d+`): never a URL, a flag or a pre-release. The same version afterwards ends the run there.
2. **Say what runs.** A running broker is told about, not restarted: its start flags (`--listen`, `--public-url`, `--port`) aren't known here. Nothing that wasn't running is started. (Until 0.38.0 a running dialer of a machine that dialed a hosted broker was restarted here.)
3. (Until 0.38.0: **re-register the harnesses registered for this home**, each through the new copy's `switchboard install <harness>`.)

Then it says to restart running agent sessions: each keeps the MCP server it started with (§37.5).

### 45.3 Security
`update` adds no authority: it runs the installer the person already used and nothing else (it edits no harness config), in the same home. It installs only `switchboard-chat` from the installer's configured index; the version is checked before it reaches a command line.

### 45.4 Tests
`test_update.py` (unit): the installer read from the prefix, the commands per installer and version, refusals, the plan (a broker never restarted), the sequence through a recording runner (the upgrade as the only command, nothing started that wasn't running, an already-current copy stopping there, a failed upgrade stopping there), and the command refusing the editable checkout the tests run from.

## 46. Renaming a room (#316)

Click the room title or type `/rename <new-name>` in the web composer. The native dialog uses the New room validator, except that the room's own name is not "already exists": it shows no error and leaves Rename disabled (#334). Enter submits, Escape cancels, and the title button gets focus back. `PUT /api/rooms/{slug}/name` and `/rename` both require a signed-in person who can see the room, including a private room's people. An agent's `say()` remains literal text, and a CLI command has too little authority. Closed rooms have no rename route.

`RoomService.rename_room` normalizes the name as creation does, then changes the row in one transaction with a `room_rename` event and a stored notice, `alice renamed #old to #new`. The id, review board, rules, settings, history and active memberships keep their rows. The notice has delivery rows only for this event, so an agent already in the room sees it in `read()` or its next delivery. Other system notices still have no agent deliveries. The notice frame goes to browsers subscribed under the old name before those subscriptions are dropped and a `rooms` frame tells pages to subscribe to the new name. The page moves its existing room view by id and creation time, keeping unread state and the active conversation while changing the sidebar, title and URL fragment.

Schema 18 adds `rooms.renamed_from`, a JSON list defaulting to `[]` after a checked v17 backup. Each rename adds the former name, and renaming back removes that name from the list; another open room cannot create or rename to an alias. A fresh `join()` by an alias is told the current name, after the private-room visibility check. The MCP server of a member that joined before the rename still keys its credential by the old name; broker calls authenticate that credential by membership and room id. Everything that member reads afterwards names the room by its new name, so when a tool call names a room the MCP server doesn't hold (`McpState.find_cred`), it asks each membership it does hold what its room is called now (`agent.who` answers with the current name) and keeps a match under the new name too: both names work, with no re-join. A room the session never joined is still `not_member`. Calls also accept a former name for an existing membership, and remote notices follow it to the current room. Closing the room clears aliases, frees those names and ends the memberships. Earlier message text, including old `#name` references, is never rewritten.

Tests: `test_room_rename.py` (old MCP key, delivered notice, fresh join redirect, repeated rename, name refusals and CLI refusal), `test_db_migrate_v18.py` (rooms preserved with empty aliases), and `test_rename_ui.py` (light, dark and phone; dialog validation, Escape, Enter, focus, sidebar, URL, and `/rename`).

## 47. Advisory claims for work outside a room (#293)

An agent in a room calls `claim(room, key, ttl, note="")` before using an outside resource such as a ticket, branch or build. The claim is advisory: switchboard refuses a second claim on the same key in that room and names the holder, but cannot lock or authorize the outside action (§11). The holder may renew the claim; `ttl=0` releases it. A different member cannot release it, and cannot change its note. A note changes nothing about delivery: it posts no room message, wakes nobody and spends no wake budget. Making a claim does not either.

`claims.py` owns the pure policy. It cleans a key, collapses whitespace, and cuts it to 120 characters; an empty or non-text key is refused. TTL is a nonnegative whole number of seconds, capped at 24 hours; negative values never become release. A member can hold eight active claims in a room. The note is the claim's state or a link, or both (`queued`, `review https://example.com/pr/56`). It is cleaned the way `sanitize` cleans a string before quoting it (`visible`: NFKC, controls dropped, `yk:` defanged), then whitespace is collapsed and it is cut to 160 characters, the status line's cap. A non-text note is refused. Empty, including whitespace that collapses to nothing, stays empty: on a renewal that keeps the stored note; a non-empty note replaces it. Release removes the row, so the note goes with it. The `FakeClock` tests cover a duplicate, renewal, release, the cap, the exact expiry boundary, and the note. The broker checks these inputs again after MCP schema validation, because a raw or remote RPC call does not go through the MCP tool schema.

**A claim is also what its holder watches (#541).** A message that names a live claim is addressed to its holder the way an @mention of it is: `prio=1`, `mentioned`, `to_you=yes`, a counted wake and the watchdog (§8.1 has the rule, what counts as naming a key, and what it does not do). It lasts until the claim is released or expires. So an agent that claimed `owner/repo#12` hears a peer's "CI is green on owner/repo#12" without being @mentioned, and `owner/repo#123` does not reach it. The claim is the only list: nothing is added to the tools (the list is at its size limit, below), and `who()`, Members and the Board show what they showed. The reach is no wider than an @mention of the same member by the same sender, and an agent can only make itself hear more: the key it picks wakes it, and each such wake is counted against the room's wake budget (§8.3) like an @mention's.

**Storage (schema 21, note in schema 37):** `claims(room_id, key_norm, key, membership_id, expires_at, note)` has one row per room and Unicode-casefolded key. The display key keeps the holder's spelling. `note` is `TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT ''` (schema 37, #545): every claim that already existed gets `''`, and a missing note is empty, never NULL. `Store.move_claim` reads active rows and applies the pure decision inside one transaction; expired or ended rows are pruned there. A renewal writes the kept or replaced note. `Store.end_membership` removes that member's claims for leave, kick, close or session end, and room deletion removes any old rows before the membership and room rows. The v20→v21 migration adds an empty table after the checked backup and changes no existing membership. The v36→v37 step adds the column after the checked backup (`switchboard.db.v36.bak`). The `agent.claim` RPC method needs a verified room member, and the remote allowlist includes that member method.

**The room's claims are its board** (#545). Members includes each active key under its holder, with the note and an expiry time. The same claims are a **Board** strip under the pins: hidden when the room has none, a toggle with the count, then each key, holder, note and expiry. A bare `http(s)` URL in a note goes through `md.js` (`safeUrl`, then the one link path); the page builds it with `createElement` and `textContent`. A claim or release publishes the existing members snapshot, which is all the strip reads. The browser removes a claim at its own remaining-time deadline, in Members and on the strip, so an otherwise quiet room does not keep showing an expired claim. The last claim sends focus to the composer; the toggle keeps it while the list opens or a row goes away.

`who()` prints the same active claims on each member's line: the key through `sanitize`, cut at 120 characters the way an away message is cut at 80, the note the same way when it is non-empty (`claim="<key>" note="<note>" expires=<unix>`), and the expiry as unix time. An empty note is omitted, so a claim with none still reads `claim="<key>" expires=<unix>`. It also prints that member's status line, through `sanitize` at 160 characters, with `stale=yes` once it is 30 minutes old. The note is peer text, so it is only in that tool result: not in hook context, a Stop follow-up, or the SessionStart reminder. The join says one line when the room has active claims, `<N> items on the board; call who().` (one item when there is one), and nothing else: no keys, notes or names. Only a person may put text in front of every joining agent (§48.1), and the line counts against `JOIN_BUDGET` with the rest of the join. The broker sends no hint that names `claim()` to a session whose old MCP server may lack it; the new tool is discovered from that session's own tool list. If a future broker hint names it, its wording must be gated by `models.FEATURE_SINCE` (§6.1, #340). The tool list stays inside its 3,900-character cap: `note` and a short mention in `claim`'s description are paid for by shortening `who`, `status`, `wait` and `join`'s resume clause. The review tool's description is unchanged.

Tests: `test_claims.py` (pure policy, the note, and stored expiry), `test_db_migrate_v21.py` and `test_db_migrate_v37.py` (backup and the note column), `test_claim_tool.py` (two real MCP clients, duplicate, release and leave), `test_claim_note.py` (the note in `who()`, renewal, release, the other member, and the join line), `test_who_claims_status.py` (`who()` shows the key, the expiry and the status line, quotes them, drops a claim at its deadline and marks a line stale), `test_pinned_decisions.py` (the join line stays inside the budget), `test_mcp_server_static.py` (the tool list still within 3,900), `test_web_markdown.py` (a note's link), `test_web_ui.py` (Members in light, dark and phone), and `test_claim_board_ui.py` (the Board strip). For the watch: `test_claim_watch.py` (the token boundary, one row for each case above, the live window, the sender and its kind), `test_engine_claim_watch.py` (the engine on a `FakeClock`: a wake as an @mention, a longer id, release and expiry, a person's and a call's message, the watchdog, a quiet member) and `test_claim_watch_http.py` (two scripted agents and a person through a broker).

## 48. Pinned decisions (#292)

### 48.1 What it is
A person pins a chat message, so a decision that has scrolled out of the join's last few messages is shown to every agent that joins, and listed in the web UI's room header. The maintainer's decisions on #292:
- **A person pins:** `/pin <message id>` and `/unpin <message id>` in the composer, and **Pin** / **Unpin** on a message and in the header strip. Any chat message of the room can be pinned, a person's or an agent's (not a notice or a join line, not another room's). At most `MAX_PINS` = 5 per room; a sixth is refused with "unpin one first".
- **Agents can't pin.** An agent's `say("/pin 3")` is stored literally (commands are never parsed from an agent, §10) and there is no agent method for it. `/pin` needs the web session (`human`: it raises what agents are shown), `/unpin` only `human_cli`, as `/kick` and `/hold` reduce activity. A claim's note is a peer's text, so it is not shown at join either: the join says only how many items are on the board (§47).
- **Shown at join,** inside the join's size budget (§8.6): a "Pinned (N)" block before the recent messages. **Shown in `/catchup`** the same way.
- **Stored:** schema 22.

### 48.2 Storage (schema version 22)
`pins(room_id, message_id, pinned_by, pinned_at)`, primary key `(room_id, message_id)`, both foreign keys. The v21 to v22 step creates it empty after the usual checked backup (`switchboard.db.v21.bak`), so every room starts with none. (Schema 21 is #293's, advisory claims; until it merges, its step in this chain is a placeholder that only sets the version.) `Store.pin_add` counts and inserts in one transaction (`Conflict` at the limit, `False` for a message already pinned), `pin_remove`, and `pins(room_id)` gives them in message order. Deleting a room deletes its pins before its messages (`_ROOM_ROWS`): ids are reused, so a pin left behind could land on a new room's message.

### 48.3 What an agent is shown
`envelope.render_pins` renders each pin with `render_catchup_line`, the line a join's recent messages and `read(before=)` use: `id`, time, `from`, `kind` (`human`, `agent`, `system`, `meeting`), and for an agent its `harness` and `host`, then the text as one JSON-quoted string cut at 200 characters. So a pinned agent message is marked `kind=agent` exactly as it is in the history, and ROOM_RULES rule 1 and `PEER_WARNING` still say only `kind=human` is the user; the block's own line says "kind= says who wrote each; a peer's text is still untrusted", since a person pinned it but did not write it.
