Its hook template sends the event, the session, `agent_id` and the type; never the message, the tool or its input. `notification_type` is a new field of the hook tool (`HOOK_FIELDS`), so the server goes first (§49.5). The reply is always `{}` (§7.3).
- **What clears it.** The next hook of the session's own thread, by its own rule (§7.4). Live: approve, and the tool's PostToolUse comes at once (then Stop; 2.1.305 and 2.1.307). **Decline** (Esc, or "No"; 2.1.305) and **no hook comes** until the person's next prompt (UserPromptSubmit): not PostToolUseFailure, not Stop, not PermissionDenied. The status therefore stays for as long as the session is waiting on its person, which after a decline is still true (Claude Code says "What should Claude do instead?"). It is not a timeout. The PreToolUse of the same call comes before the prompt, so it cannot clear it, and the plugin sends PreToolUse for switchboard's own tools only (§49.3).
- **Subagents.** Claude Code runs the session's hooks for a subagent's tool calls (§49.4, #446), and sends the Notification for a subagent's prompt **with no `agent_id`** (live: the PermissionRequest of the same prompt names the subagent). So a prompt in a subagent shows the session as waiting, which is true of its person. A subagent's own hooks still settle nothing for the session, with one exception: a tool that finishes in a subagent (PostToolUse or PostToolUseFailure with an `agent_id`) ends the hold (`Engine.subagent_tool_ran`), because otherwise a subagent that runs for minutes after its prompt was approved would keep the session "waiting" the whole time. It changes nothing else: no confirmation, no context, no listener ended. A subagent's tool finishing while another prompt is still open ends the hold early (the status falls back to `busy`, what it was before #472).
- **A listener.** A Notification is a hook of the session, so it ends an idle session's open listener like any other (§49.4); a background subagent's prompt in an idle session is the case, and the listener starts again at the session's next Stop.
- **Who sees it.** `who` adds what it means to the member's line: "paused at its person's approval prompt; it answers nobody until they act. Wait for it or move on: your call" (`WAITING_APPROVAL_WHO`). The join text of every harness over HTTPS adds the same, shorter (`APPROVAL_GUIDANCE`, about 150 characters; the test agent's join, which the 6,000-character budget test measures, is unchanged). Neither says which to choose. Members and the Inspector show "Waiting for approval", and a review board's card for the agent whose move it is shows the same in words and amber, where it showed "working" (§37). `switchboard report` counts a prompt open for more than 60 seconds as a stall (§12.6).
- **The other harnesses: Codex and Devin report it too, with a hook that cannot decide.** Codex 0.158.0 and Devin CLI 3000.11.92 fire PreToolUse and then **PermissionRequest** at an approval prompt and nothing else (Codex also an Interrupt when the prompt is declined with Esc; Devin nothing); neither has a Notification event, and Devin's did not fire. Their only hook is therefore a permission hook, which was left unregistered until the maintainer's yes of 2026-10-21 (§11 item 2): asked whether to add a report-only PermissionRequest hook for them that "can never approve or deny anything", the answer was "Do this". It reuses this section's path: `asks_approval` in both adapters (`ev.ev == "PermissionRequest"`), the same status, the same clearing, the same `who` and join text. Cursor Agent 2026.10.18 has no event at an approval prompt at all (21 events; its import of Claude hooks maps PermissionRequest and Notification to nothing; `beforeShellExecution` and `beforeMCPExecution` run before every command, prompt or not), so it still looks `busy`, and docs/USAGE.md says so.
- **Why a command that discards the reply (checked live, Codex 0.158.0 in a throwaway `CODEX_HOME` and Devin 3000.11.92 with a project-level config in a temp directory).** A PermissionRequest hook's output is the permission decision in both harnesses. Codex's docs say an `mcp_tool` hook has "the same output contract as a command hook", and it does: with a stand-in server that answered the hook tool with `{"hookSpecificOutput":{"hookEventName":"PermissionRequest","decision":{"behavior":"allow"}}}`, Codex ran the command with no prompt. So a plugin hook of the kind every other switchboard hook is (`mcp_tool`, whose output is the server's reply) could be made to approve by a hostile server, and the report-only rule would hold only while the server was honest. The same decision printed by an `async` command hook (after 2 s) did nothing: the prompt stayed and the command did not run, as Codex documents ("can't block, approve, rewrite, or otherwise control"). Devin CLI 3000.11.92 does the same with a command hook: one that printed `{"decision":"approve"}` had the command run with no prompt (its docs define a top-level `decision` and don't say what a PermissionRequest hook's output does). And the shipped hooks, run by the real Codex and the real Devin against a server that answered every report with an allow and an approve, left the prompt as it was. So both plugins' hooks are commands that print nothing, and `/mcp/event` is their only door: the `hook` tool answers `{}` and does nothing for a PermissionRequest (an `mcp_tool` hook of this event is refused, not served).
- **What the hooks are.** *Codex:* `plugins/codex/bin/approval`, a command hook in `hooks/hooks.json` (`"command": "\"$PLUGIN_ROOT\"/bin/approval"`, `async`, `timeout` 5), POSIX `sh` and `curl`. It takes the thread's id from the payload with `grep` (and sends it only if it is letters, digits, `-` and `_`), posts `{"harness":"codex","event":"PermissionRequest","session_id":…}` to `$SWITCHBOARD_URL/mcp/event` with the token on curl's stdin, sends curl's output to `/dev/null`, prints nothing and exits 0. The plugin is 0.4.0; Codex asks the person to review its hooks again. *Devin:* `bin/hook permission` (`plugins/devin/lib/hook.py`, `report_permission`): the same request with `"harness":"devin"`, 2 s at most, every error swallowed, nothing printed. The harness gives the hook the tool and its input (`tool_input.command` and the rest); neither script forwards them. Both need `SWITCHBOARD_URL` (the server's address without `/mcp`) and `SWITCHBOARD_AGENT_TOKEN` in the harness's environment: Devin's other hooks already did, and for Codex it is one more line in the Connect card (Settings → Agents) and USAGE. A Codex set up before this, with the plugin updated and no `SWITCHBOARD_URL`, works as it did and isn't shown as waiting.
- **What the server does with a report.** `CommandHook` (`/mcp/event`) takes `harness: codex` for this one event (any other event, and `/mcp/pretool`, are refused with 400), reads the session id of the body and no other field, and answers `200 {}` in every state, whatever happened to the session. `AgentService.http_hook` drops any output the engine hands a report-only event (`models.REPORT_ONLY_EVENTS`) and acknowledges nothing; the engine hands none, in any state of the session (`tests/unit/test_engine_core.py`). A report from a session that never joined is inert.
- **What clears it, per harness (live).** *Codex:* approve, and the command runs and the tool's PostToolUse comes (status → `busy`), then Stop (→ `idle`); decline with Esc ("You canceled the request"), and no hook comes, so the status stays until the person's next prompt (UserPromptSubmit → `busy`). Codex runs a subagent's tool hooks with the thread's session id and the subagent's own turn id (§49.4, #446), so a prompt in a subagent shows the thread as waiting, and the subagent's finished tool is the only sign that it was answered: `Engine._claim_for_hook` hands a hook that `other_thread` recognises to `Engine.subagent_tool_ran` (it ends the hold and does nothing else), as the HTTP layer does for Claude Code's subagents (and, as there, a subagent's tool finishing while the thread's own prompt is still open ends the hold early). This case is tested in the engine and not live: asked to run a command through a subagent, Codex's model declined to. *Devin:* approve, and the command runs; the config's group for every other tool (#493, §49.7) reports the tool at once, so the status ends at the tool's PostToolUse (live: 0.06 s after the person approved, 0.8 s before Stop). Before #493 Devin's hooks ran for switchboard's own tools only and nothing of Devin's reached the broker until its next switchboard call or its Stop, so a long approved command followed by many other tools kept the status for as long as the turn ran. For both, a report that reaches the server after the prompt was already answered (a slow network and a quick person; Codex's hook is `async`, so it never holds the prompt) shows until the session's next hook. Decline with Esc ("Canceled due to user interrupt"), and no hook comes, as for Codex.
- **Tests.** `tests/unit/test_engine_core.py` (the hook sets the status and only for the right type; each next hook clears it by its own rule; the message that waited arrives in the clearing hook; a subagent's tool ends the hold and nothing else; for Codex and Devin: PermissionRequest sets it, every next hook clears it, the engine answers a report with nothing in every state, the other harnesses take no PermissionRequest, a Codex subagent's finished tool ends the hold), `tests/unit/test_permission_hooks_static.py` (every plugin's hook file is read: only Codex's and Devin's register a permission hook, one each, as a command, `async` in Codex; both scripts run against a server that answers with every decision it can and print nothing, exit 0, send the session id and the event's name and no tool or input, and neither hangs on a silent server), `tests/integration/test_approval_report_only.py` (the server's half: the report sets the status and is answered `{}`, in every state with a message waiting; only the session id is read; Codex's door takes this event and no other; the `hook` tool refuses it; whatever the engine hands back is dropped), `tests/integration/test_agent_plugins.py` (the plugin's Notification group, its fields against the recorded request and payload; Claude Code's and Cursor's plugins register no PermissionRequest; end to end with the recorded request: Members, `who` and the join text, the held message, the clearing PostToolUse; other types ignored; a subagent's Notification ignored), `tests/e2e/test_web_ui.py` (Members, the Inspector and the board card, on a phone too) and `tests/e2e/test_agent_tokens_ui.py` (Codex's Connect steps give the address and the token).

### 49.10 Signing an agent in through the browser (#386)

Decision 1 of #380 made a token per device first and browser sign-in later. This is later, for the two harnesses whose MCP client does OAuth: Codex (`codex mcp login`) and Claude Code (`/mcp`, Authenticate). The person types and copies nothing; the agent starts the sign-in, and the person approves it in the browser. The rules are `broker/oauth.py`, the routes are in `web.py`, and the consent page is `web/static/oauth.html` and `oauth.js`. **What an agent ends up with is an agent token (§49.1)**: nothing about `/mcp` changes after it is made, and an agent that uses a token by hand is untouched, and so are the `curl` hooks of Cursor and Devin, which cannot use OAuth. Claude Code's listener and session report can't either, and use a key of their own (§49.11).

**What the clients do** (a spike against a logging stand-in server, 2026-10-21; then both against a real broker, below). Claude Code 2.1.305 and 2.1.307, after a 401 with `resource_metadata`, and Codex 0.158.0, after a bare `GET /mcp`, read `/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource`, then `/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` at the root; register by dynamic client registration (`token_endpoint_auth_method: none`, `application_type: native`, `grant_types` `authorization_code` and `refresh_token`, and a loopback redirect: Claude Code `http://localhost:<port>/callback` and a client name of "Claude Code (<server name>)", Codex `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/callback/<random>` and "Codex"); send the browser to the authorization endpoint with PKCE `S256`, `state` and `resource=<the /mcp URL>`, and no `scope`; and swap the code with a form-encoded `POST` of `client_id`, `code_verifier`, `redirect_uri` and `resource`. Neither used client ID metadata documents, and **both took a token answer with no `expires_in` and no refresh token** and called the server with it. Claude Code skips OAuth for any server that has a static `Authorization` header (see "Claude Code and the plugin" below).

**The flow**
1. An agent with no live token calls `/mcp` and is told, in the 401's `WWW-Authenticate`, where its metadata is (§49.2).
2. `GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource` (and the same at `/mcp`, RFC 9728) names this server's `/mcp` as the resource and itself as its one authorization server. `GET /.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` (RFC 8414) says what it does: `S256` only, `none` for client authentication, `authorization_code` only. Both are built from the web origin (§30), never from the request's Host.
3. `POST /oauth/register` (RFC 7591): `oauth.register` stores a name and the redirect URIs and answers a `client_id` (`sbc_` and 16 characters), with no secret. Other metadata is read for errors and then dropped.
4. The agent opens `GET /oauth/authorize?…` in the person's browser. The broker checks the request (`oauth.parse_authorize`). A client it does not know or a `redirect_uri` the client did not register gets a 400 page and no redirect; any other mistake (no PKCE or `plain`, another `response_type`, a `resource` that is not this `/mcp`, a bad `state`) is sent back to the agent's registered address as `error=…&state=…`, so it stops waiting. A good request gets the same static page for everyone, and, when the browser has no session, a sealed `switchboard_oauth_next` cookie (HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict, Secure behind https, ten minutes) holding the query. Signing in lands on `/`, which sends the browser on to `/oauth/authorize` with that query, once; the cookie is only a way back, because the consent page and every route behind it check the request again.
5. `oauth.js` asks `GET /api/oauth/consent?<the same query>`, which checks the request again and answers `signed_in: false` (a 200: the page asks again every three seconds, so a sign-in in another tab, or `switchboard login` on a desktop, carries it on, and a refused request would be a console error each time), or who is signed in, what the agent calls itself, where it will answer, and whether a password or passkey check is still wanted.
6. **The consent card** says: who is signed in; what the agent calls itself (its own claim, shown as text); that its answer goes to "a program on this computer" at a loopback address; a **Name this device** field (the rule of Settings → Agents, checked in the page and again in the broker); what approving grants (act as your agents on that device; not sign in here, post as you or run a command; approve only if you just started this) and where to revoke it; **Approve** and **Deny**. On a hosted broker with no password or passkey check in the last five minutes, a Confirm it's you step (the password, or **Use a passkey**) comes first, as for Make a token (§31.6, §32.4).
7. **Approve** is `POST /api/oauth/approve {query, device}`. It needs a person's session (a cookie: an agent token is no session), the page's `Origin`, and `X-Switchboard: 1` (the same rule as every web write, §5.4), a fresh check on a hosted broker, a request that passes `parse_authorize` again, and a device `agent_tokens.free` accepts (a host name, not held by anyone's live token or sign-in, the person under their cap of 20). It makes a code and answers `{redirect}`: the registered address with `code` and `state`. The page opens it only if it is a loopback address (`http://localhost|127.0.0.1|[::1]:<port>/…`), whatever the broker said. **Deny** (`POST /api/oauth/deny`) answers `access_denied` and the state the same way and makes nothing.
8. `POST /oauth/token` (`oauth.exchange`): the code, its PKCE verifier, the client and the redirect it was made for, and a `resource` if one is sent. It makes the device's agent token (`agent_tokens.make_signin`: `via='oauth'`, the agent's name) and answers `{access_token, token_type: "Bearer"}`, `no-store`. It answers 400 with RFC 6749's `error` and `error_description` otherwise (`invalid_request`, `invalid_grant`, `invalid_target`, `unsupported_grant_type`).

**Codes** are 32 random bytes, kept in memory only as their sha256 (`OAuthState`), good for 60 seconds, once, at most 256 at a time. A code is **one try**: asking for it removes it, so a wrong verifier burns it. A code presented again after it was spent revokes the token it made (RFC 6749 §10.5) and ends that device's sessions, and says so in the `agent_tokens` events (`why`). A restart forgets every code, which costs a person one more click.

**Clients** (`oauth_clients`, schema 31) are only needed for the few minutes a sign-in takes, so `oauth_client_add` removes the ones older than a day and the oldest past 500 as it adds one. Registration takes no sign-in, so it is limited to 60 a minute for everyone (429 `temporarily_unavailable`); someone who can reach the server can therefore keep new agents from registering for a minute at a time, the same class as flooding sign-ins (§32.6). A body is at most 8 KB, read with a cap before it is parsed. Only **public clients** register (`none`, never a secret), and **only loopback redirect URIs**: `http://localhost`, `http://127.0.0.1` or `http://[::1]`, an explicit port and a path, at most 200 characters, no user info, query or fragment, up to five. So a link someone sends cannot make the browser deliver a code to another host. The `redirect_uri` of an authorize request must be one of them **as a whole string**: another port, a trailing slash, another case, or the same address by another name is refused, which is what Codex's random path segment needs and what no port wildcard would give. Client ID metadata documents are not supported: the server would have to fetch a URL the client names.

**The token** is `sbat_…` like any other, kept as a sha256, one live per device name and counted against the person's cap of 20, listed in Settings → Agents beside the typed ones (`signed in from Codex · made … · last used …`) and revoked by the same button, which ends the device's sessions (§49.3). Removing a person revokes theirs (§49.1), and a code made for a person who was removed in the meantime is refused. **There is no refresh token and no expiry**, like a typed token's: the hooks and Claude Code's listener hold a fixed token anyway, a rotation would add a race between the sessions on one machine, and the control is Revoke. A revoked sign-in makes the agent's next call a 401 with the metadata, and the agent asks its person to sign in again (Codex: `AuthRequired`, `codex mcp login switchboard`). A name that a token or sign-in already holds is refused, never replaced, so Claude Code and Codex on one machine sign in under two names.

**Guards.** `OAUTH_PATHS` (`auth.py`) is a second, two-path list beside `AGENT_PATHS`: `POST /oauth/register` and `POST /oauth/token` are exempt from `HostOriginGuard`'s rule that an unsafe request carry the page's Origin, because the callers are programs that send none; each instead **refuses any request that has an `Origin`** (a browser's script always does) and reads no cookie, as `/mcp` does (§49.2). The route-enumeration test (`test_no_unauthenticated_write_routes`) passes unchanged for that reason. Every other route of this section (`/oauth/authorize`, `/api/oauth/*`) keeps the full rule, and the CSP is unchanged: the consent page is a static file with a same-origin script, its Approve is a `fetch`, and the hand-off to the agent is a navigation (the policy's `form-action` does not apply). The page cannot be framed.

**Threat model delta** (to §31.6 and §49.1)
- **Nothing a room message or another agent can do approves a sign-in.** Approving is the person's click on a page that only a signed-in browser's cookie, Origin and header open: a link in a room opens the consent page and nothing more, an agent token is refused where a session is needed, and a request from another site fails the Origin rule. What the person is shown is the broker's own check of the request, not the agent's description of it.
- **A consent phishing link** (a client registers as "Claude Code" and sends the link) can only deliver its code to a loopback address on the machine the browser runs on, and the code is useless without the verifier only that client holds. The page says what approving grants and to approve only a sign-in the person just started; what is shown is the name the client gave itself and never an identity.
- **A stolen web session cannot mint access this way** on a hosted broker: approving needs the same password or passkey check in the last five minutes as Make a token. On a desktop the owner signed in from the terminal, so nothing more is asked, as for Make a token.
- **The code travels in a URL** to a loopback address and is single-use, short-lived, and bound to the PKCE verifier, the client and the redirect. `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` holds on every page. No code, verifier or token is logged, stored in the clear or put in an event.
- **The agent's name is a claim:** it is cut to 80 printable characters, shown with `textContent` only, and decides nothing.

**Claude Code and the plugin.** The shipped plugin cannot do OAuth in Claude Code, because its `.mcp.json` sets `Authorization: Bearer ${user_config.token}`, and Claude Code (2.1.305 and 2.1.307) never starts OAuth for a server with a static `Authorization` header: "OAuth fallback is disabled when headers.Authorization is set", also with an unset token (the header is then `Bearer`) and with an `oauth` block in the server's config. A plugin's `headersHelper` can send no header when there is no token, but its environment has none of the plugin's own settings (`CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_*` are not set for it), so it cannot read the token. Claude Code's idle wake (`bin/listen`) and its SessionStart and SessionEnd reports (`bin/session`) are command hooks that need a token whatever the connection uses, so Claude Code with only a sign-in is woken by its hooks after a tool call and by its `wait()`, never by the listener, and a session that ends is not reported (its member keeps its last status). **Claude Code also refuses to send its credentials to a token endpoint that is not https**, unless the host is `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` or `::1` ("Refusing to send credentials to non-https token endpoint"; seen live against `http://switchboard.localhost:<port>`, a desktop broker's own address), so it can sign in only to a hosted broker behind https. Codex has no such rule, and everything that runs through its own connection (its Stop hook and its tool hooks) works with a sign-in; only its approval-report command hook (§49.9) needs the token and `SWITCHBOARD_URL` in the environment. The plugin was therefore **not changed by #386**. #488 changed it (§49.11): the typed token moves to `X-Switchboard-Token`, so there is no `Authorization` header to turn the sign-in off, and the two scripts present a listener key instead of a token.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/test_oauth.py` (the rules: redirects, the request check, codes and their lifetime and cap, PKCE against RFC 7636's vector, registration limits), `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v31.py` (the step, on a 0.37.2 database), `tests/integration/test_oauth_signin.py` (the whole flow on a desktop and a hosted broker as Claude Code and Codex drive it, and everything that must not work: PKCE and redirect mistakes, a code used late, twice or for another client, who may approve, the two routes refusing a browser, the way back after signing in, people's own sign-ins), and `tests/e2e/test_oauth_consent_ui.py` (the card, Approve and Deny handing the answer to a loopback address, Confirm it's you, signing in first, an invalid request, the phone, markup in an agent's name, and the page refusing a non-loopback address). **Live, 2026-10-21 (throwaway brokers in temp homes, headless Chromium as the person, throwaway config directories for each harness):** Codex 0.158.0 against a desktop broker (`codex mcp login`, Approve on the card, `codex exec` joined #build and posted as `codex@work-laptop`), and Claude Code 2.1.307 with a header-less copy of the plugin against a broker whose public URL is `http://localhost:<port>` (`/mcp` Authenticate, Approve, `claude -p` joined #build and posted as `claude@claude-box`, its PreToolUse hook naming the session). After a Revoke in Settings, Codex's next call failed with `AuthRequired` and Claude Code's tools were gone ("needs authorization"); signing in again needs the same command.

### 49.11 Claude Code signs in through the browser: the listener key (#488)

§49.10 made browser sign-in work for Codex and for Claude Code's own connection, and left Claude Code's plugin out, for two reasons. The plugin sent its token as a fixed `Authorization` header, which turns Claude Code's OAuth off. And its two command scripts, `bin/listen` (the idle wake) and `bin/session` (a session's start and end), talk to the server on their own and need a token, which Claude Code keeps to itself when it signs in. This section is what lets one plugin serve both ways: a Claude Code session with no pasted token signs in through the browser, joins a room, is woken while idle and goes offline when it quits. It adds a new kind of credential, the **listener key**, so §11 (items 9 to 12, and the residual risks) and SECURITY.md say it too.

**What Claude Code does** (2.1.307 against a logging stand-in server, and then against a real broker, 2026-10-22, in throwaway config directories):
- **An `Authorization` header turns OAuth off, whatever it holds.** A plugin whose token option was unset sent `Authorization: ` (empty), and Claude Code never read the server's metadata. A header with another name doesn't: with `X-Switchboard-Token: ` empty it went straight to the discovery documents and registered.
- **A plugin's MCP server may name a `headersHelper`**: a command whose printed JSON object becomes headers of that connection, and the one thing that carries data made on the machine into the signed-in connection. It runs once as the connection starts, before the SessionStart hooks (5 ms before, in `claude -p`), with the plugin's folder as its working directory and `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}` and `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA}` filled in on its command line. Its output is sent on every request of the connection, the OAuth discovery ones too. It gets the environment Claude Code was started with and not the plugin's settings (as #386 found), and the `CLAUDE_PID` and `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID` in it are whoever started Claude Code's, not its own. Its parent is the `sh -c` that Claude Code runs, and that shell's parent is Claude Code itself: the pid its command hooks get as `CLAUDE_PID`. A helper that exits 1, prints junk or prints nothing does not stop the server from connecting.
- **On Windows it runs the helper with `cmd.exe`** (2.1.308 on Windows 11, with Git for Windows installed as well): `cmd.exe /d /s /c "<command>"`, because it starts a `headersHelper` with Node's `shell: true`, whatever shell the plugin's hooks use (Git Bash, or PowerShell where there is no Git). `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}` and `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA}` are filled in on the command line with forward slashes (`C:/Users/…`), its working folder and `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT` are the plugin's, and `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA` is not in its environment. Its parent is the `cmd.exe`, and that one's parent is `claude.exe`, the pid the hooks get as `CLAUDE_PID`. The hooks' own chain under Git Bash is `claude.exe`, `bash.exe`, a forked `bash.exe`, `sh.exe` and PowerShell, and Windows' process list loses a link of it (MSYS's fork leaves a parent that no longer exists), so nothing started from there can find Claude Code by its parents. A command written for the hooks' shells, `cd "…"; ./headers "…"; exit $LASTEXITCODE`, which #488 sent, ended in 45 ms under `cmd.exe` with no header (`cmd.exe` takes `;` and `./` differently): Claude Code logged that the helper returned no valid value, and connected without a key.
- **An `mcp_tool` hook can't give a script anything.** Its answer is read as hook output, and text on `UserPromptSubmit` is context for the model; Claude Code skips `mcp_tool` hooks at `SessionStart`, before MCP is up. A key handed down a hook's answer would reach the model and the transcript, and nothing would exist before the first turn. So the key is **made on the machine and claimed over the signed-in connection**, never handed down it.

**The typed token's place.** The plugin's token option stays, and is no longer required. `.mcp.json` sends it as `X-Switchboard-Token: <token>` (empty when none is typed) and never as `Authorization`, and the server takes an agent token in either (`AgentAuth.token`: `Authorization: Bearer sbat_…` first, then `X-Switchboard-Token`; the first live one wins; a listener key is neither). So a setup with a typed token connects as ever and Claude Code starts no sign-in for it, and one with none is answered 401 with the metadata (§49.2) and offers Authenticate in `/mcp`. A hand-made `claude mcp add --header "Authorization: Bearer …"` is untouched. The cost: a header that a proxy doesn't redact by default as it does `Authorization`, so DEPLOY.md says to redact it; the broker itself logs no header. **An older server ignores the new header, so the server must be upgraded before the plugin** (§49.5; the release notes say so).

**The key.** `bin/headers` (POSIX `sh`; Windows below) runs as the connection starts. It makes `sbl_` and 64 hex characters from 32 bytes of `/dev/urandom`, keeps it in `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA}/listener-key-<Claude Code's pid>` (`umask 077`, a temporary file in the same folder renamed over the name, so a reader never sees half a key and a link planted at the name is replaced, never followed), removes the files of pids that no longer exist, and prints one header: `X-Switchboard-Listener: <the key's sha256>`. The pid is the script's parent's, or its parent's parent when that is a shell, which is the `CLAUDE_PID` the hooks get; the file is named for it so each Claude Code on a machine has its own key, and a key a hook finds is its own Claude Code's. Whatever stops it (a folder it can't write, no way to hash, no `/dev/urandom`), it prints `{}` and exits 0, so the connection simply has no key and the scripts do nothing, as before.

**The key on Windows (#499).** The helper is `bin/headers.cmd`, which `cmd.exe` finds for the one command in `.mcp.json`, `"${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}/bin/headers" "${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA}"` (a quoted path and a quoted argument: all that `sh` and `cmd.exe` read the same way), and which starts `bin/windows/headers.ps1` in Windows PowerShell 5.1 with the profile skipped and the execution policy lifted for that run, as the hooks' `.cmd` files do (§49.5). The script does the POSIX helper's job: 32 bytes from the system's generator (`RandomNumberGenerator`), `sbl_` and 64 hex, the sha256 printed as `X-Switchboard-Listener`, a temporary file renamed over `listener-key-<pid>` (`Move-Item -Force`: a link or a hard link planted at the name is replaced, never written through), the files of pids that no longer exist removed, and `{}` with exit 0 for anything that goes wrong (a relative folder, a folder it can't write, a refused `Add-Type` under constrained language mode). Two things differ, because Windows has no `umask` and no `$PPID`:
- **The file is private by its access list.** The script makes the temporary file empty, replaces its access list with one rule for the signed-in user (`FullControl`, `Allow`) with inheritance off, so none of the folder's rules apply, reads the list back and gives up (`{}`) unless it is protected and every rule is the user's, and only then writes the key into it and renames it. On Windows 11 the list of the file a real session used: protected, one rule, the user, not inherited. As on POSIX, any process of the same user can read it, and Administrators can take it, as root can read a 0600 file.
- **Claude Code's pid** is the first ancestor of the script that is not a shell (`cmd`, `powershell`, `pwsh`, `bash`, `sh`, `dash`, `zsh`, `conhost`), which on Windows is `claude.exe` (seen: the script, then the `cmd.exe` that runs it, then `claude.exe`, the pid the hooks get as `CLAUDE_PID`). A parent that started after its child is a pid reused since and ends the search, as a parent that is gone does. The parent comes from one call to `NtQueryInformationProcess` on a process handle opened for query only (`Add-Type`, about 0.2 s), not from CIM: `Get-CimInstance Win32_Process` took 2.5 s a question on the maintainer's machine, and a walk with a question per hop 10 s, where the helper has a time limit. All of it takes about 1.1 s under Windows PowerShell 5.1 and 2.2 s under PowerShell 7. Off Windows (the tests run the script under `pwsh` on Linux and macOS) the parent comes from `ps`, and `chmod 600` stands in for the access list.

`bin/windows/listen.ps1` and `session.ps1` use the key as `bin/listen` and `bin/session` do, by the same rules (below), reading at most 256 bytes of the file, shared for reading, writing and deleting so that the helper can replace it while a listener reads it. A Git Bash that runs `bin/headers` itself gets `{}`.

**Claimed over the sign-in.** `AgentAuth` takes the header from a request that carries a **live agent token**, which is the browser sign-in's, and `ListenerKeys.claim` records the hash in `listener_keys` (schema 33: the token, the hash, the one session it is for, made, last used), after checking it is 64 lowercase hex. Only a request with a live token claims a key, so there is **no unauthenticated route** and nobody can claim one ahead of the real connection; a hash another token holds is never taken over. A request writes at most once a minute (the claim is remembered), and the table keeps at most 16 keys a token (a connection makes a new one each time it starts: the oldest unused go first) and drops any unused for a week.

**What a key can do.** It is a bearer for exactly three routes, as `Authorization: Bearer sbl_…`: `POST /mcp/listen`, `POST /mcp/session` (SessionStart and SessionEnd only, as for a token, §49.4) and, since #623, `POST /mcp/meeting/host`, where the script that hosts a call registers it (§54). The third widens what a key may do, so the maintainer was asked on 2026-11-05, with the cost stated as "one more address on the server accepts the plugin's script key", and said yes ("Yes to all three"). `AgentAuth.door` is the only place that takes one, and all three routes call it. Every other door answers an agent token only and says 401 to a key, in either header: `/mcp` (every tool, and the hook tool, so a key calls none and can't make a call for a session), `/mcp/pretool` and `/mcp/event`, and a hosted call's own three routes, which want the call's key (§54). The web UI's routes and the socket never read one. It is for **one session**, so a key found by another process, or one that outlived its session, can't wake, report or end any other:
- A hook of the signed-in connection that carries the key's hash names the session (`HttpTools.on_hook` calls `name_session`, with the hook's `session_id`), and that is the session the key is for. A `/clear` or a resume in the same Claude Code makes a new session, and its first hook moves the key. A hook of another connection never moves it (the key is found by its hash and its token).
- Before any hook has run, which is how a resumed session's listener starts (hooks are skipped at SessionStart), the key's own first request names the session and pins it, once.
- A request for any other session is refused 403 `wrong_session`, on the listener and the session route. The registering route (§54) does not take the session from its request at all: the key's own is read from the server's record (`ListenerKeys.session_of`), a key that is for none yet is refused (409 `no_session`) and not pinned by it, and a body that names another session gets the same 403.

**Rotated and revoked.** Every connection makes a new key. A key lives only as long as its token: revoking the sign-in in Settings → Agents, and removing its person, end it with the token, and it then answers 401 `revoked` (never a different word for a key nobody claimed, which is `unknown_key`, so an unclaimed hash and a guess look the same). It also ends after a week without use (a listener round and every request of the signed-in connection count). The table is kept across a broker restart, because an idle session's listener has nothing to claim a key again with.

**What the scripts do.** With a token typed, as ever, whether or not a key file is there. With none, `bin/listen` and `bin/session` (and `listen.ps1` and `session.ps1` on Windows) read the key from the file named for `CLAUDE_PID`, and only a key's shape (`sbl_`, 64 hex) is sent: anything else in the file is no key. The credential reaches `curl` on its stdin, or in a request header the PowerShell scripts build themselves, never on a command line, as the token does.
- **A fresh session, before any key exists** (#413's wake-before-first-turn). The listener at `startup` and `resume` starts as Claude Code connects, so the file may not be there yet, and the signed-in connection's first request, which tells the server about the key, can come a little after the listener's first: the server then answers 401 `unknown_key`. The listener at SessionStart therefore waits up to a minute for the file and, while the server doesn't know the key, asks again every second, as a first round each time. A session whose sign-in is saved gets its key known within a second, so it parks and an @mention wakes it before its first turn. A person who never signed in has a key the server never hears of: after the minute it ends quietly, with a line in `listen.log` and no wake, because waking a session nobody asked to be reachable would start a turn in it. A Stop's listener doesn't wait for either: by then the key is known or never will be, so it ends at once, with a line in `listen.log` too. A `claude -p` run (`CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ATTENDED=0`) starts no listener at all (§49.4), so the key matters to it only for the session report. `bin/session` at SessionStart tries once and doesn't wait, because the listener reports the session when it parks, and the session script must not hold Claude Code's start-up.
- **Each round reads the key again**, so a reconnect that made a new key is picked up.
- **A revoked sign-in** (401 `revoked`) is a refusal the session must hear, once, like a refused token (§49.4): the status and the error word, never the rest of the body, with the same once-only marker, and what to do (sign in again with `/mcp`, then Authenticate). **A key for another session** (403 `wrong_session`, which is what the listener of a session that a `/clear` ended gets) is ordinary and is only logged. A 401 or 403 with no such word, a proxy's, is told as for a token.

**Threat model delta** (to §49.1, §49.10 and §11)
- **A listener key is less than the token it came from.** It can listen for and report the start and end of one session, so what it gives is that session's wake text (a message the room would show it anyway) and the power to take it offline. Since #623 it can also register a hosted call (§54) for a room that session is in, at five tries a minute: a call that is the person's, limited as §54 says, that the person or an admin ends from the call bar. It can't call a tool, post, read a room, sign in or run a command.
- **It is kept where the plugin's other state is**, a 0600 file under the user's home that any process of the same user can read, the agent's own shell included, as a typed token's copy in the environment is. What a prompt-injected agent gets from it is less than what it already has in its own tools. Another user of the machine can't read it (the file is 0600, in a folder under the user's home), and the server never has the key, only its hash.
- **No unauthenticated claim.** A request that carries a hash and no live token is refused as any other, and writes nothing, so a key can't be planted ahead of the real connection, and a hash seen in a proxy's log is no use without the key it came from.
- **A key isn't a token.** The two are told apart by their prefix and shape (`sbat_` and `sbl_`). The listener and session routes take either, `/mcp` and every other door take a token only, and a key put in either token header is no token. The header that carries the key's hash carries no secret.
- **The header's own hash is public**, so a second connection could send the same hash to try to make the server forget or move the key: it can't claim a hash another token holds, and moving a session needs a hook of the token that holds it.
- **The helper is code on the machine**, as the plugin's other scripts are: a fixed script of the plugin's folder that reads nothing of the user's and writes one file in the plugin's data folder.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v32.py` (the step, on a 0.37.2 database), `tests/unit/test_listener_keys_rules.py` (claim, pin, move, revoked, idle, the cap, with a `FakeClock`), `tests/integration/test_listener_keys.py` (the headers on a signed-in connection as Claude Code 2.1.307 sends them: only a live token claims; the key at the listener and the session route; refused everywhere else; one session and how a hook moves it; revoked; kept across a restart; a typed token in either header and refused to a browser) and `tests/integration/test_claude_plugin_signin.py` (the three scripts run as Claude Code runs them, once as `sh` scripts and once as their PowerShell twins under `pwsh` where it is installed (#499): the helper's file, mode, hash, pid, cleanup, links, spaces, and what it does when it can't; the listener's and the session script's key use, wait, retry, quiet and told endings, the credential never on a command line; and the whole way against a broker, a resumed session woken before its first turn with the key unknown at first). `tests/live/test_live_claude_signin.py` (opt-in, `SWITCHBOARD_LIVE=claude`; the driver is `tests/live/harness/claude_signin.py` and a hosted mode of `harness/world.py`) is the real Claude Code, with no token typed, against a throwaway hosted broker on `http://localhost:<port>`, in a throwaway config directory with a copy of the account's login only: `/mcp`, Authenticate, a headless Chromium as the admin approving the consent card, then join, an idle wake through the key, the key shown to open no tool and no other session (401, 403 `wrong_session`, 401 `unknown_key`), quit shown offline, a resume woken with nothing typed, and a revoked sign-in ending the member. **Live, 2026-10-22** (Claude Code 2.1.307, `claude --model haiku`): all six passed in 32 s (9 model calls), the idle wake and the resumed one by `stop_followup` in 2.4 s and 3.1 s; the existing typed-token run (`test_live_claude.py`) passed against the new plugin and server too (7 in 31 s). Along the way: the key file is `listener-key-<pid>` with the pid of the `claude` process, mode 0600; the plugin's server shows "needs authentication" until `/mcp` signs it in, and a `claude` with a saved sign-in connects on its own at the next start.

**Tests on Windows (#499).** `tests/integration/test_claude_plugin_windows.py` keeps what only Windows does without a Windows machine: the helper's command reads the same in `sh` and `cmd.exe` (a quoted path and a quoted argument and nothing else) and is the one in `.mcp.json`; `headers.cmd` starts the script with the data folder quoted, ASCII, CRLF; the script's source makes the file private before the key is in it (an empty temporary file, then an access list of the user alone with no inherited rule, read back and refused unless that is what it is, then the key, then the rename), grants no other account, starts no process but `ps` and `chmod` off Windows, puts the key on no command line and uses no network, and finds the pid from its ancestors with one `ntdll` call and no CIM; the PowerShell scripts stay ASCII and free of PowerShell 7-only syntax; `.gitattributes` gives each file its line endings.

**Live on Windows, 2026-10-22 (#499)**: Claude Code 2.1.308 (`claude.exe` from the Claude desktop app) on Windows 11, with Windows PowerShell 5.1.26100, PowerShell 7.4.21 and Git for Windows, against a throwaway hosted broker on `http://127.0.0.1:<port>` reached through a tunnel, with a stand-in for the model API (it answers "ok" and, for one prompt, calls `join`), a throwaway `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` and plugin copies under the temp folder (all deleted afterwards), and nothing of the real Claude configuration touched. No token typed. `/mcp`, Authenticate, a headless Chromium as the admin approving the consent card, and the callback pasted into Claude Code's own "paste the URL" prompt (the card's redirect goes to a port on the Windows machine): "Authentication successful". Then: the helper ran under `cmd.exe` and left `listener-key-<claude.exe's pid>`, whose list was protected with one rule, the user's; the signed-in connection's next request claimed the key; `join` (through `ToolSearch`, as a model does); an @mention woke the idle session with its text in the next request to the model (`Stop hook feedback`, no turn typed; the stand-in never answers in the room, so the engine asks again once, `again=yes: unanswered`); `/exit` took the member offline within about 3 s; a restart with the saved sign-in connected on its own and, with `--continue`, was idle within 3 s of starting and woken by an @mention before any prompt was typed; and revoking the sign-in ended the parked listener with the server's 410 line, told to the session once, then 401 `revoked`, logged. Natively as well, with a stand-in for Claude Code (a copy of `cmd.exe` under another name, which is not a shell) and the hook's own command lines in Git Bash, Windows PowerShell and PowerShell 7: the key made by the helper under `cmd.exe /d /s /c`, claimed by a connection that carried its hash, the listener woken (exit 2, the message on stderr) and `SessionEnd` taking the member offline, with no token in the environment, the files or any command line. The script's replacement of a file held open for reading (with deleting shared: replaced; without: `{}`, the old key kept), of a planted hard link (the victim file unchanged) and of dead pids' files was run in both PowerShells. **Not checked:** a symbolic link at the key's name (creating one needs a privilege the account didn't have), PowerShell as the hook shell inside a real `claude.exe` (the same command lines were run directly, in both PowerShells), Windows 10, a machine without Git for Windows, constrained language mode, and a Group Policy that sets the execution policy (it overrides `-ExecutionPolicy Bypass`, as for the hooks, §49.5).

**Codex browser-signed-in hosting (#637).** Codex 0.166.0 has a local [`http_headers_helper`](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/extend/mcp), a command printing a JSON header map for a locally connected HTTP MCP server. The user explicitly installs `bin/headers-launcher --install` (Windows: `bin/windows/headers-launcher.ps1 --install`) from the normal personal `.codex/plugins/cache/switchboard/switchboard/<version>` cache. It copies a stable launcher beside the versioned folders, at `.codex/plugins/cache/switchboard/codex-headers` (Windows: `.cmd` and `.ps1`), and never changes Codex config. The user configures that fixed absolute path in their existing server entry. The launcher resolves the highest numeric release version from its own location, never an environment path; verifies cache components and helper ownership, no links and no shared write access (Windows also permits SYSTEM and Administrators); and checks the bounded plugin manifest's name and version before running `bin/headers` (Windows: `bin/windows/headers.ps1`). Invalid candidates are skipped; no valid candidate emits `{}` with a diagnostic on stderr. Custom cache layouts are unsupported. `meeting-host plan` and `status` read only the bounded `config.toml` helper setting, never execute it or print its values, and diagnose a missing configured executable, with a repair instruction (#665). This is independent of the plugin's hooks. It emits only the existing `X-Switchboard-Listener` header, never an Authorization header, so saved OAuth and a typed token keep their normal precedence. It reads no harness credential store. This option is not for an executor-backed remote connection.

The helper makes 32 random bytes with the same `sbl_` prefix and keeps the key as `codex-listener-key-<Codex process pid>` in `SWITCHBOARD_PLUGIN_DATA` or `~/.cache/switchboard` (an explicit helper argument overrides it). POSIX uses umask 077, a 0600 temporary file and atomic rename; Windows sets and verifies an account-only access list before writing, as Claude's helper does. The helper's process parent, skipping its shell, names the file. Reconnecting rotates it; dead processes' files are removed. Failure emits `{}` and leaves the MCP connection's own authentication alone.

`AgentAuth` already claims the header hash only with a live token. On an authenticated Codex request, `HttpTools.prime` pins that key to the **first thread in the connection's own MCP metadata**, not to hook arguments or a host-script body. Subsequent threads (subagents included) cannot move it, nor can a Claude hook. **Schema 44** adds `listener_keys.harness`, NOT NULL, default `claude`, checked to be `claude`, `codex` or the closed `unknown` state; every existing key keeps its old session and Claude scope. A newly claimed hash starts unknown and opens no script route. The authenticated request's existing initialize/discover client metadata identifies its harness, which cannot then change; a bounded 8 KiB inspection leaves the MCP body's own limits unchanged. The MCP call context also identifies a pending key before binding its thread. The migration uses ALTER after the usual verified backup and does not rebuild the table or widen the 128-character session limit. Both harness and session are required to match: equal raw ids on different harnesses never confer authority.

For a Codex key, the only usable script route is **`POST /mcp/meeting/host`**, already one of Claude's key routes. It reads the stored harness and session; the existing optional `session` field is only a consistency check. The host script finds this process's key by walking its own ancestors, at most 32 hops, and sends its bounded `CODEX_THREAD_ID` in that field, so a subagent shell cannot register with its parent's key. It never searches another process or Claude's files. The room must contain a live member of that exact Codex thread on the sign-in's device. A key with no authenticated thread yet is `no_session`; a different thread is `wrong_session`. Revocation, idle expiry, retention and rate limits are the existing key and registration rules.

Codex keys are refused at `/mcp/listen` and `/mcp/session`, including with the same raw session id; those routes still serve the existing harnesses only. They cannot call a tool or hook, report approval, read room text, or use a call key's routes. **No route, request field, tool, hook reply shape, `AGENT_PATHS`, `HOOK_FIELDS`, `SESSION_FIELDS`, or listener harness allowlist widens.** The persisted harness binding tightens cross-harness scope. `SWITCHBOARD_AGENT_TOKEN` remains first choice for the Codex host; no Claude credential is a fallback. The shared sh and PowerShell hosts stay byte-for-byte equal in both independently installed plugin roots.

The threat is the existing same-user file risk: a process of that user, including a prompt-injected shell, can read the key and occupy its own thread's room/person call slot by registering a useless call hash. It gains no other session, tool, wake text, or OAuth credential. The caller's normal permission prompt still covers running containers. Tests: `test_db_migrate_v44.py`, the Codex case in `test_listener_keys_rules.py`, `test_codex_listener_keys.py`, and the helper and key-host cases in `test_meeting_codex_host.py` (sh and PowerShell where installed).

**Live verification (#637, Codex 0.166.0).** The seven-scenario run sets `http_headers_helper` in its temporary `CODEX_HOME`. It made a 0600 process-key file, and a test-only observer at the broker's HTTP boundary saw 52 MCP requests carrying the valid hash header, matching that private file, with no raw script key in the headers. Registering with that key for its bound thread returned 200; naming another thread returned 403 `wrong_session`. Wake, mid-task delivery, subagent isolation, quit, resume and revoke passed; no prompt was approved, no protected configuration drifted and no process remained. This harness uses an agent token for MCP authentication, not Codex's browser sign-in; the browser-sign-in token path is covered by `test_codex_listener_keys.py`, with no token in the host script's environment. The Claude eviction regression in `test_listener_keys.py` recreates an evicted key as unknown, then confirms that the next authenticated tool context reclassifies it and its listener receives a wake.


### 49.12 OpenCode V2 over HTTPS (#513)

**Scope and measurements.** [The maintainer's approval on #515](https://github.com/amahpour/switchboard/pull/515#issuecomment-6373360741) reverses prior-art §8.1: OpenCode belongs on the HTTPS path. It also approves `POST /mcp/status`, its `AGENT_PATHS` and method/field allowlist additions, and the existing Stop-park pattern with queue-mode wakes. OpenCode **2.1.4** was run with `opencode run --standalone`, temporary `OPENCODE_CONFIG_DIR` and inline `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT`, against a local logging server using the broker's FastMCP **4.0.9**. Real `initialize`, `tools/list` and `tools/call` requests are in `tests/fixtures/mcp_http/opencode_*.json`: the initialization proposes **2025-11-25**, which FastMCP returns unchanged. Both default Code Mode (the model uses `execute`) and `codemode: false` native calls carry **`params._meta["ai.opencode/sessionID"]`**. No protocol override or Code Mode restriction is needed. The default client name is `opencode` with version `2.1.4`. OpenCode loads skills from `.opencode/skills` and, for compatibility, `.claude/skills` and `.agents/skills`. This plugin is the status and idle listener, not one of those directories, and it does not register the review skill (#629, §37.4).

**Identity and adapter.** `HttpTools.prime` reads that metadata, bounded like Codex's thread ID; no session context means a refusal naming the missing field, never an MCP-connection or last-session fallback. `HttpsOpenCodeAdapter` is a `Listening` adapter: `opencode@https`, tier `opencode:https`, key `opencode@<device>:<session id>`. The token authenticates the person and device; the ID is only the client's claim. OpenCode has permission rules, not a mode, so `reports_approval_mode` is false. `HOOK_CONTEXT_EVENTS["opencode"]` is empty: busy sessions hear messages through `read()`, `wait()` or `say()`, without tool-context injection. Join guidance says so.

**Status route.** `POST /mcp/status` (`OpenCodeStatus`, `AGENT_PATHS`) accepts a live agent token through `AgentAuth.token`, refuses any Origin, ignores web cookies and refuses listener keys. It is OpenCode-only, with fixed method, event and field allowlists (`OPENCODE_STATUS_EVENTS`, `OPENCODE_WAKE_EVENTS`, `OPENCODE_STATUS_FIELDS`), a 4 KiB body cap and bounded IDs. Unknown fields, event names and shapes fail closed. Adding these entries constrains a new route; existing guards are unchanged. Execution started maps to UserPromptSubmit/busy; succeeded, failed and interrupted to Stop/idle; session deleted and location shutdown to SessionEnd/offline. The two tool hooks enqueue reports without awaiting the per-session chain or any network request; that chain preserves before/after order even when a broker stalls. Successful switchboard tool calls acknowledge only their extracted batch tokens, with no input or result text sent. Code Mode fires an inner hook for each actual MCP tool (`switchboard_read` etc.); the plugin never scans its `execute` aggregate. Status replies are always `{}`, even for permission events; the plugin does not speak MCP.

**Approval hold.** `permission.asked` marks the broker member waiting-approval and immediately aborts the plugin's local listener. Ordinary status events, tool reports and listener rounds cannot override that hold. The plugin checks `ctx.permission.list` read-only before reporting further activity and again before a wake; an unavailable query fails closed. An empty list permits the explicit `permission.cleared` report, including after a plugin restart. No `ctx.permission.reply`, permission rule or permission hook is registered by the shipped plugin. The permission action, resources and tool input never leave OpenCode.

**Idle wake.** `plugins/opencode/index.ts` uses V2 `Plugin.define` from `@opencode/plugin`; `listener.mjs` handles status and the listener. It posts to `/mcp/listen` with the session and `X-Switchboard-Harness: opencode` (absent still means Claude Code). The broker uses exactly its existing Stop park and envelope. For OpenCode only, its 200 response carries `X-Switchboard-Batch` and the existing one-use hook-ack nonce in `X-Switchboard-Ack`, and HTTP receipt does not acknowledge the continuation. The plugin posts `wake.queued` only after `ctx.session.prompt` admits the text, or `wake.dropped` when it discards the body. These two events accept exactly `event`, `session_id`, `batch_id` (positive integer below 10¹²) and `ack` (32 lowercase hex characters). The token/device/session and exact outstanding nonce must match. A queue receipt uses the existing hook-ack and confirmation machinery; a drop uses expiry to return the batch to pending. Late or foreign receipts do nothing. Until admission, unrelated execution starts, tools and MCP calls cannot confirm it. Even a first OpenCode round is refused when busy, offline or waiting-approval, so a racing round never resets an approval to idle. 200 with nonempty text queues exactly that text through `ctx.session.prompt`, `delivery: "queue"`, `metadata.source: "switchboard"`; peer text remains the existing “call read()” stub. Both queue and steer started an idle 2.1.4 session in a standalone measurement (new execution within 3 ms of admission); queue preserves order if the person types concurrently. Once 200 headers arrive, becoming busy does not cancel the body or suppress queue admission; only a pending permission, lost ownership, deletion or shutdown does. 408 starts another round after 1 s; 409 and 401 stop; unreachable, 429 and transient 5xx back off from 1 s to 60 s. Redirects are refused. A 200 body cut by a proxy retries too. An OpenCode reconnect may recover an unconfirmed `stop:followup` offer (the broker has marked it busy in anticipation, but no execution or tool has confirmed it); an actual busy turn, approval hold or offline state still refuses it. A synthetic listener Stop, idle report or approval hold expires an unadmitted continuation instead of confirming it; the next eligible round can carry it again. A missing receipt expires through the normal no-ack timer. No new wake text format is introduced. The listener bounds its wake text to 64 KiB and starts at most one prompt for a response; a lost prompt admission answer is never retried.

**Location and lifecycle.** The service broadcasts every location's events to every plugin instance. Each action checks `ctx.session.get(...).location.directory` against this instance's `ctx.location.directory`, using canonical paths when their spellings differ (macOS's `/var` and `/private/var`). OpenCode 2.1.4 emits an ID-only deletion event after `session.get` is gone: that tombstone may report offline only for a session whose ownership was already verified, never authorize a wake or create ownership. A known move clears that ownership. The plugin cancels unanswered parks when a turn starts and cancels even a received body when permission is asked, the session is deleted or the location unloads; cleanup reports its own sessions offline. A real `location.shutdown` carries its directory in `event.location.directory`, with empty `data`; that event uses the same canonical directory comparison as session ownership. It checks ownership again after a wake arrives and before submitting it. A fork with a new ID becomes a separate member. Continuity across a move is not inferred, and the previous location cannot wake the moved session. A forced process kill has no guaranteed offline report; the last status can remain until the next report or token revocation.

**After a restart or a reload (#520).** A session that was idle when the plugin loaded (`opencode service restart`, OpenCode quitting and starting again, a change under the plugin's folder, an upgrade, a crash) emits no event until its next turn, so before this it was not listened for, and after a reload its old instance's `location.shutdown` left the member offline. At setup the plugin now asks `ctx.session.list({directory: ctx.location.directory, limit: 20})` in the background, and for each listed session that no event or tool hook has named yet it does what an idle report does: the ownership check, `ctx.permission.list`, the report (`session.execution.succeeded`, with `permission.cleared` first for a session whose permissions it has not yet read), then the listener round. The broker's side is unchanged: the same `/mcp/status` events and `/mcp/listen` round, no new route, field or event, and no new default. A session in no room accepts the reports, which do nothing, and its round is refused with 409, which ends that listener. It is bounded: at most 20 sessions (`SCAN_LIMIT`), skipping one that says it was last updated more than a day ago (`SCAN_AGE_MS`), one that is mid-turn and one that names another location, one at a time, and all in the background, so neither `setup` nor a tool hook waits for it, and a plugin that is closed mid-scan sends nothing more. Whatever an event or a tool hook says since the plugin started wins over the list. A foreign location or a pending permission is handled as ever (nothing sent, or `permission.asked` and no listener). **What the answer looks like (measured on OpenCode 2.1.4).** `ctx.session.list({directory, limit})` returns `{data: [...], cursor}`; a row has `id` (`ses_...`), `projectID`, `model`, `cost`, `tokens`, `outcome`, `time` (`created`, `updated`, `idle`, in milliseconds), `title` and `location` (`directory`). An idle row has `outcome` (`succeeded`) and `time.idle`; a row mid-turn has neither. **Which rows are idle (`listedSessions`).** A row is taken when it has an `outcome` or a `time.idle` that is not before its `time.updated`. A row whose time carries `created` or `updated` but has neither `outcome` nor `time.idle` is mid-turn and skipped, and so is one whose `time.idle` is before its `time.updated` (it has worked since it last idled), even when an `outcome` is still set: the two contradict, so the row is not trusted. A row with no time at all is kept, as nothing says it is busy. A row whose `location.directory` is present and is not this plugin's location (compared as `own` does, by canonical path) is left out before the cap of 20 is counted, in case OpenCode ignores the `directory` argument; a row with no location is checked by the ownership check as ever. `data`, `items`, `sessions` or a bare array are accepted, an id as `id` or `sessionID`, and a time only if it is a number (seconds or milliseconds); an answer it does not recognise, a throw or a missing method lists nothing and logs one line (`could not list sessions`), and the event path works as before. Running sessions are not told apart any other way (`GET /api/session/active` is a server route the plugin has no handle on), so a session that is running but shows no sign of it in its row would be reported idle, which only makes Members say idle until its next event, and a wake it is given queues behind the turn (`delivery: "queue"`). The live test does not yet restart a private instance.

**Credentials and setup.** The token comes only from `SWITCHBOARD_AGENT_TOKEN`, stays in memory, and is sent only in the HTTP header: no subprocess, credential file, log or command argument. Both environment variables are required: without `SWITCHBOARD_URL` the plugin sends no status and cannot wake a session. HTTPS is required except for loopback desktop addresses, including `switchboard.localhost`. Settings → Agents gives the V2 `mcp.servers` block, plugin path and environment lines, as does USAGE. Copy this release's `plugins/opencode/` into the project and restart OpenCode; no package publication or V1 support is implied. Browser sign-in is out of scope for this plugin.

**Database.** Schema 34 adds only `opencode` to participants' harness CHECK. SQLite's documented schema-text procedure for a CHECK change leaves its rows, indexes and referencing tables intact, keeps foreign keys enabled, advances SQLite's schema cookie, reloads the parsed schema, and runs the existing integrity and row-count checks before commit. Each migration step declares whether it edits schema text and the SQL fragments that must appear afterward; an unmatched replacement raises `SchemaError`, resets writable-schema mode and rolls back to v33 with the verified backup intact. It runs inside the normal migration transaction after the verified backup. Schema 35 (#518) makes the same edit to the `agent_icons` CHECK, so an admin can upload an avatar for OpenCode (§29.11); until they do, it shows switchboard's own angle-brackets glyph (§29.9).

**Tests.** `tests/integration/test_agents_http_opencode.py` replays both recorded call shapes: two sessions, join/say/read/wait, missing metadata, revoked tokens, status and listener doors, field/event allowlists, the peer-stub wake, dropped-body redelivery without false confirmation, genuine busy refusals, queue-admission receipts, human-message recovery after a drop, approval holds and offline events. `tests/opencode_plugin_harness.mjs` drives the shipped listener under Node: 200/408/409/401/unreachable, backoff, cancellation and a racing approval, canonical and foreign locations, bounded wakes, token-only acknowledgments, no credential in logs or argv, and the recorded shutdown event stream. `tests/unit/test_permission_hooks_static.py` additionally scans all shipped OpenCode JS/TS sources: only `ctx.permission.list` and the read-only `realpath` fs import are allowed, with no subprocess imports or file writes. Outside comments and strings the name `permission` may appear only in `ctx.permission.list(`, and no string may be exactly `permission`, so a destructured, renamed or aliased `permission`, bracket access and `Reflect.get` are refused too (#517). `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v34.py` checks the migration on a real old fixture and its backup. The Agents card is covered by `tests/e2e/test_agent_tokens_ui.py`. `tests/opencode_plugin_harness.mjs` also drives the startup list (#520): idle, mid-turn, foreign-location, permission-pending and stale sessions (rows in the measured shape), the cap of 20 and a bare array, events winning over the list, a list that never answers, and an unavailable or unrecognised one; `tests/integration/test_agents_http_opencode.py` replays a reload (`location.shutdown`, then a startup's reports) and a mention waking the session. `tests/live/test_live_opencode.py` is opt-in (`SWITCHBOARD_LIVE=opencode`), runs a real standalone CLI with a temporary HOME and XDG directories and only a provider key inherited, and never reads or writes real OpenCode config or calls the background service. Its test-only driver keeps that private server alive while the target session joins, is woken and has a permission request held; it never answers one.


## 50. Quiet: wake only for what is addressed (#548)

One agent in one room can be quiet: ordinary unaddressed chatter never starts a turn for it. Explicit listening (§52.1) separately opts into call chatter. The switch is off until a person turns it on, so a membership that is never set behaves as §8.2.

**Stored per membership.** `memberships.quiet` is `INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` with `CHECK(quiet IN (0, 1))` (schema 36). There is no unknown mode. Existing rows migrate to 0, after the usual checked backup (`switchboard.db.v35.bak`).

**What counts as chatter.** `rules.unaddressed_chatter`: an agent's message, priority 0, and not a reply to this member's own message. A person's message is priority 2. An @mention, `@here` and `@everyone` are priority 1. A reply to the member is priority 0 with `reply_to_me`.

**What changes while it is on.** `rules.chatter_due` takes `addressed_only` from `memberships.quiet`. The hold's expiry is then false, and an open `wait()` (where `hold_s` is 0, so every chatter item would otherwise count as addressed) treats only replies as addressed. Concretely:

- no wake after `unaddressed_hold_s`;
- a Stop whose only pending wake is that chatter does not answer the hook. A listener (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, OpenCode) keeps its park open. Devin has no park: the Stop does not `decision: block` with the chatter. It takes the path it already takes when there is nothing to deliver, a re-arm that says to call `wait()`, or nothing when the re-arm caps are spent;
- the chatter does not end an open `wait()`;
- `rules.chatter_wakes_at` is `None`, so Members does not say "wakes at" and the Inspector shows no **Wake now** for it. The budget-exhausted notice does not fire for it either: the budget is not what holds it.

It still rides the next real wake. A person's message, an @mention and a reply release as they do today, and `releasable` sends the pending chatter along with them, as any other wake does. `/wake <name>` still forces the waiting chatter out: the person asked, the same way a person's message skips the hold. The next `read()` still shows it, whole. Mid-task delivery is unchanged (§8.2): a PostToolUse answer is still priority only, and unaddressed chatter is not added there.

**Who sets it.** Only a person, by two commands (§10). `/quiet <name>` turns it on. It reduces what wakes the agent, so the role is `human_cli`, the same as `/hold`. `/unquiet <name>` turns it off. It raises activity, so the role is `human`, web only, the same as `/release`. Both re-evaluate that member: turning it on stops a chatter release the hold has already earned, and turning it off delivers chatter that is already past the hold. An agent's `say("/quiet …")` is stored as literal text, as every other command is. No tool sets the flag.

**The page.** `member_dict` carries `quiet`. The Inspector's actions row has **Quiet** / **Unquiet** beside Hold. It posts `/quiet <name>` or `/unquiet <name>`, with `aria-pressed`, and its title is "Quiet: wake <name> only for messages addressed to it". A quiet member is marked "quiet" where a held member is marked held, in Members and on the Inspector.

## 51. Admins (#569)

The owner was the only admin. Once someone is an admin they are an admin: they can do everything the owner can. The one guard is the owner. Nobody can remove the owner, and nobody can take the owner's admin away.

**What an admin is.** Schema 38 adds `people.admin INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK(admin IN (0, 1))`. A new person is 0. Removing them sets it back to 0, and someone added again later is a new row, so they start as an ordinary person. The owner has no `people` row (§32.2), so the flag lives on `Who`: `admin` is the row's value, and `is_admin` is `owner or admin`. `owner` stays for what is the owner's alone: their own password and passkeys, `name_locked`, and the claim. A web session stores only its id hash, and `who_of` reads the row on every request, so taking admin away is seen on the next request.

**The route.** `POST /api/people/{pid}/admin` with `{"admin": true}` or `{"admin": false}`, and nothing else. An admin only; a desktop broker is 404, as the other people routes are. Making someone an admin needs the fresh check (§32.4), because it grants access. Taking it away needs none, because it only takes access away (§32.3). `pid` `owner` or `0` is 403, "nobody removes the owner or the owner's admin". An unknown or removed person is 404. A body that isn't exactly that JSON boolean is 400. An admin may take their own admin away; the owner is always there. Setting the flag to the value it already has changes nothing. A change writes a `people` event (`what: admin`, who, whom, the new value) and a notice in every room that names who did it ("alice made bob an admin", "alice took bob's admin away"), then `people_changed`.

**The guard.** Every gate that used to test `Who.owner` for the admin section now tests `is_admin`: `admin()`, the avatar routes (the same check the owner had, renamed), `GET /api/me`'s `admin` and `can_set_icons`, and `GET /api/agent-icons`'s `can_change`. On a desktop broker `admin` stays false, because there is no people list; `can_set_icons` stays true, because the one person is the owner. Whatever a route lets the owner do to anyone, the owner's account included, it lets any admin do. The fresh check stays on the routes that grant access and off the ones that only take it away. `POST /api/people/owner/remove` stays refused.

**The frame.** `people_frame` gains `admins`: the names of everyone who is an admin, the owner first. An open page whose own person joins or leaves that list refetches `/api/me` and redraws, so the sidebar's Admin entry, the People sheet and Settings → Appearance → Agent icons appear or go without a reload. If the People sheet is open when this person's admin is taken away, it closes. A name that isn't in the frame's `people` list was renamed, and that is not admin being taken away. Who is an admin is no secret among signed-in people. The frame still carries no email.

**The page.** The owner's card has an **Owner** badge and no button that changes it. Every other admin's card has an **Admin** badge. Every other card has **Make admin** or **Remove admin**. Your own card has only that button: no **Remove** and no **New one-time password**, which would sign you out with no way back in (your own password is in Settings). Making someone an admin goes through Confirm it's you. The card shows the result or the error, and keeps focus and drafts the way the other rows do. On a phone the badges and the buttons wrap, so the sheet does not scroll sideways.

**Threat model, a delta to §32.6.** Every admin is as trusted as the owner. Any admin can take over any account but the owner's admin, by setting its email or giving it a new one-time password. They can also upload the agents' avatars and read every private room's name (§44.4). The owner cannot be removed from that position. A stolen session still cannot grant admin: that needs a fresh check. Taking it away does not.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/test_db_migrate_v38.py` (a real schema-37 database, from the 0.37.2 dump migrated to 37, gains the column at 0, and NULL and 2 are refused; the backup is that schema-37 file). `tests/unit/test_people_admins.py` (`is_admin`, and `summary`'s `admin` and `owner`). `tests/integration/test_admins.py` (the fresh check, then everything the owner can do, a second admin, taking it away on the next request, the owner's email and names, the owner refused, a person who isn't an admin refused, the notice, the event and the frame). `tests/e2e/test_admins_ui.py` (the Owner badge, Confirm it's you, a second page gaining and losing Admin without a reload, no button on the owner's card, only Remove admin on your own, no sideways scroll on a phone).

## 52. Meetings: a live call in a room (#573)

A room can follow one live call. [joinly](https://github.com/joinly-ai/joinly) (MIT, image `ghcr.io/joinly-ai/joinly:v0.5.4@sha256:d19ca294eb24625d69d8069d0168cb5214af3652fbf721eb07480177eb4b9ea0`) joins Zoom, Meet or Teams through a headless browser and transcribes with Whisper. switchboard is its client over streamable HTTP. The bot listens. It speaks, and shows a picture of the call, only when a person has asked (**Voice** and **Snapshots**, below). It does not share a screen. A call can also be hosted on a person's own machine, which pushes its lines in instead of the broker pulling them: §54.

**Config.** `[meeting] joinly_url` is that MCP URL (`http://127.0.0.1:8010/mcp/` when joinly is published on localhost as [DEPLOY.md](DEPLOY.md) says). It comes only from `config.toml` or the environment variable `SWITCHBOARD_JOINLY_URL`, never from room text. The variable exists for a Kubernetes manifest (and Argo CD with it), which has no file on the volume to write; as for `SWITCHBOARD_HUMAN_NAME`, a non-empty value wins over the file and an empty one is unset. The variable goes through the key's checks (`http` or `https`, 300 characters, a host, no user name or password), and a bad one stops the broker at start with `SWITCHBOARD_JOINLY_URL must be an http(s) URL …`: it is never ignored, because a typo would turn `/meeting` off with no word of why, and the message does not repeat the value, which may carry a password. Empty means `/meeting <link>` answers with a short hint that names DEPLOY.md and both ways to set it, and no row is written. `[meeting] tts_voice` (default `bm_george`) is the Kokoro voice joinly speaks with: a word of 1 to 32 characters from `a-z`, `0-9` and `_`, or the config is refused. It is checked only for its shape, because joinly owns the list.

`[meeting] max_calls` (#607, default 1) is how many calls the broker follows at once, across all rooms: an integer from 1 to 50, or the config is refused (a bool, a string, 0 and 51 are all errors). One joinly serves several calls at once, one MCP session each, but a live call costs it about 2.7 GiB and one CPU (measured, #604), so the number has to match the memory the bot is given. `SWITCHBOARD_MEETING_MAX_CALLS` sets it from the environment, for a manifest or a chart with no file to write, and as for `SWITCHBOARD_JOINLY_URL` a non-empty value wins over the file and an empty one is unset. The variable is a whole number written in ASCII digits, 1 to 50, and anything else stops the broker at start with `SWITCHBOARD_MEETING_MAX_CALLS must be a whole number from 1 to 50`: it is never ignored, because a typo would leave the limit at 1 (or the file's) with no word of why. Like the URL it comes only from the file or the environment, never from room text.

`[meeting] stt_model` (#617, default empty) is the Whisper model joinly transcribes with. It is an allowlist: `base`, `small`, `distil-large-v3` or `large-v3-turbo`, or empty. Anything else is refused when the config loads, with a message that names the four, and that includes a name with the wrong case or padding, a path, and a Hugging Face repository id, which faster-whisper would take as readily as a size and fetch: what joinly downloads is never left to a typo, and the four are the ones the proof of concept measured or sits beside. A value that is not a string is refused too. `SWITCHBOARD_MEETING_STT_MODEL` sets it from the environment, for a manifest or a chart with no file to write; as for the other two, a non-empty value wins over the file, an empty one is unset, and anything but the four names stops the broker at start with `SWITCHBOARD_MEETING_STT_MODEL must be one of base, small, distil-large-v3, large-v3-turbo`, never ignored, and without repeating what was given.
